## M.3 getIOFile answers null for a failure and null-dereferences the subfile it extracts

`src/ctrmap/formats/containers/AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:247` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `byte[] b = getFile(fileNum); File out = new File(files.scratch(), "agfc_extract_" + UUID...); OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(out); os.write(b); ... } catch (IOException ex) { return null; }` - getFile is documented as returning null, and os.write(null) is a NullPointerException that this catch does not cover. The catch that IS here discards the reason entirely (no log, no message). Callers do not check: Builder.java:576 does `Files.copy(currentAGFC.getIOFile(index).toPath(), ...)`.

**What the user gets.** Export a subfile the container cannot read and the Extras archive browser dies on an NPE from a null path; a genuine write failure into scratch returns null with the reason destroyed, and the user is told nothing at all. This is the boolean-that-nobody-reads shape item I removed from storeFile, still present one method away.

**Fix.** Throw with the container and subfile named, as storeFile now does, and refuse a null subfile before opening the output.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what was claimed, and I could not refute any leg of it. 1) The defect, verbatim. src/ctrmap/formats/containers/AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:247-258: `public File getIOFile(int fileNum){ try { byte[] b = getFile(fileNum); File out = new File(files.scratch(), "agfc_extract_" + UUID.randomUUID().toString()); OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(out); os.write(b); os.close(); return out; } catch (IOException ex) { return null; } }` `os.write(b)` at line 252 dereferences b (FileOutputStream.write(byte[]) reads b.length), so b == null is an NPE, not an IOException, and the catch at 255 does not cover it. 2) getFile really does answer null, by design and by its own javadoc. Same file, lines 204-241: the javadoc at 205 says "One subfile, whole, or null", and the body returns null at 217 (bad bounds), 221 (want < 0) and 239 (`catch (IOException e) { Logger...log(Level.SEVERE, ...); return null; }`). Line 224 re-opens the origin file on every call (`new FileInputStream(f)`), so verify()'s construction-time offset check (lines 159-172) does not protect a later read: a container that was valid when opened and is then deleted, locked or truncated — the exact scenario storeFile's own javadoc names at line 274, "an emulator or a virus scanner holding the archive open" — gives FileNotFoundException/EOFException, null, then NPE. 3) No caller handles it. src/ctrmap/hum

## M.4 getFile answers null for a subfile it could not read, and 57 of 80 call sites feed that null straight into a parser

`src/ctrmap/formats/containers/AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:215` - **api-trap** - effort: large

**What the code does.** `public byte[] getFile(int fileNum)` returns null at three points - bad index, negative length, and `catch (IOException e) { ...log...; return null; }` - and its own javadoc says "One subfile, whole, or null". Exactly one caller refuses it: `CameraDataFile(AD ad)` does `byte[] input = ad.getFile(3); if (input == null) throw new IllegalStateException(...)` (CameraDataFile.java:41-45), which is the fix shipped as OUTSTANDING item J. Of 80 `getFile(n)` call sites in production, 57 have no null test within five lines, and the unchecked ones are the load path: `new ByteArrayInputStream(f.getFile(0))` (MapMatrix.java:60 and :91), `new ByteArrayInputStream(this.mapFile.getFile(2))` (GRCollisionFile.java:47), `new ByteArrayInputStream(f.getFile(3))` (GRPropData.java:27), `new ByteArrayInputStream(mapFile.getFile(0))` (Tilemap.java:86), `new ZoneHeader(data.getFile(0), game)` / `new ZoneEntities(data.getFile(1))` / `new GFLPawnScript(data.getFile(2))` (Zone.java:38-40), `new BCHFile(file.getFile(1))` (TileMapPanel.java:261), `new BCHFile(new MM(staged, files).getFile(0))` (NPCRegistry.java:87).

**What the user gets.** `new ByteArrayInputStream(null)` throws NullPointerException, which is not an IOException, so it escapes every one of those constructors' `catch (IOException)` arms. A truncated or locked container - the case the readFully work in OUTSTANDING item A was measured against - turns into a bare NPE with no message naming the archive or the subfile, thrown from inside a zone load or a matrix load. The user sees a stack trace on a console they do not have, or a dialog whose reason is the word "null".

**Fix.** Give the container the same treatment storeFile got: `public byte[] requireFile(int num)` that throws IllegalStateException naming the file and subfile, and make it the only public reader - `getFile` either goes away or becomes package-private for the two places that genuinely test for absence. Every one of the 57 sites then either compiles against a method that cannot answer null, or has to say in its own words why absence is acceptable.

**Verified.** The three null returns are on the lines claimed: AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:217 (`fileNum + 1 >= offsets.length` -> null), :221 (`if (want < 0) return null`), :239 (`catch (IOException e) { Logger...log(SEVERE, "reading " + f + " subfile " + fileNum, e); return null; }`), under a javadoc at :205 reading "One subfile, whole, or null". The sole refusing caller is CameraDataFile.java:41-45 (`byte[] input = ad.getFile(3); if (input == null) throw new IllegalStateException("the area holds no camera table (subfile 3 is empty)...")`) - and that shipped message is the proof of reachability: an AD with fewer subfiles than the caller asks for is a case this project has already met in the field, so the index-out-of-range null is live, not theoretical. Unchecked sites I opened and read: ZoneHeader.java:260-261 `byte[] wTex = areadata.getFile(11); if (wTex[0] == 0x11)` - a raw dereference in fetchArchives, which has no try block at all, and its neighbour at :253 goes through Bytes.checkBCHMagic, which at Bytes.java:97 does `data.length` with no null test, so both NPE; GRCollisionFile.java:47 builds `new ByteArrayInputStream(this.mapFile.getFile(2))` OUTSIDE the try that opens on :48, so nothing catches it; GRPropData.java:27 and Tilemap.java:86 sit inside `catch (IOException)` arms an NPE escapes; Zone.java:38-40 tests nothing. Two of the census's citations are wrong and I am not endor

## M.5 The provably game-invalid legacy collision writer is still public, callable, and guarded only by a comment

`src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionBounds.java:25` - **api-trap** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `public void updateBounds(GRCollisionFile f)` (GRCollisionBounds.java:25), `public void write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos)` (GRCollisionBounds.java:103), `public void write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos)` (GRCollisionMesh.java:39) and `public GRCollisionMesh[] computeMeshOrder()` (GRCollisionFile.java:200) have ZERO call sites anywhere in src/ (grep over all 264 production files plus the battery; the only `.write(dos)` hits inside them are their own inner calls to structs[i]/tris.get(i)). They are the old serializer: GRCollisionFile.write() now does `mapFile.storeFile(2, GfColl.build(tris, null));` (line 162), and its own javadoc at lines 143-149 says the in-class math "was PROVEN game-invalid (bounds mismatch retail in 846/857 regions, bucketing misses whole-cell triangles in 853/857 - guaranteed height-lookup holes) and must never be used for writes again." GfColl's class javadoc repeats it: "all collision WRITES must go through this class." Dead support state hangs off them too: GRCollisionBounds.originalExtremes (line 11, assigned at GRCollisionFile.java:83 and GRCollisionBounds.java:28, never read), GRCollisionFile.offsets/lengths (lines 29-30) and unknown_const_0x5D8_1/0x0/0x5D8_2/0x1 (lines 25, 34-36) - all written at parse, never read.

**What the user gets.** Nothing in the language stops the next edit (or a merge, or a well-meaning "restore the bounds before saving") from calling updateBounds()+computeMeshOrder()+write(dos): the whole path compiles and looks like the writer. The bytes it produces mismatch retail in 846/857 regions and lose whole-cell triangles in 853/857, which in game is a walkable floor with holes in the height lookup - the player falls through or cannot cross. This is the rule the project measured and then left enforced by prose.

**Fix.** Delete the legacy serializer outright - updateBounds, GRCollisionBounds.write(dos), GRCollisionMesh.write(dos), computeMeshOrder, originalExtremes, offsets/lengths and the four unknown_const fields - so that reaching for it is a compile error rather than a code review. If GRCollisionBounds must stay for the parse, drop its write side entirely so the type cannot serialise at all, and leave GfColl.build as the only thing that can produce a coll subfile.

**Verified.** I tried to refute it and could not: every line the census pointed at says what it claims. `public void updateBounds(GRCollisionFile f)` is at src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionBounds.java:25 and `public void write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos)` at :103; `public GRCollisionMesh[] computeMeshOrder()` at src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionFile.java:200; `public void write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos)` at src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionMesh.java:39. Call-site greps over all of src/ return exactly one hit each - the declaration itself (`grep -rn "updateBounds" src/` -> only GRCollisionBounds.java:25; `grep -rn "computeMeshOrder" src/` -> only GRCollisionFile.java:200). The two write(dos) methods have no external callers either: the only `.write(` hits in the whole gfcollision package are their own inner calls (GRCollisionBounds.java:105 `structs[i].write(dos);`, GRCollisionMesh.java:41 `tris.get(i).write(dos);`), and no code outside the package touches `f.bounds` at all. The real writer is GRCollisionFile.write() at :150-163, which flattens the 16 bucket lists and does `mapFile.storeFile(2, GfColl.build(tris, null));` (:162) - it never reads `bounds`, `offsets`, or the unknown consts. The only enforcement that the legacy math must not be used is prose: GRCollisionFile.java:140-149 ("was PROVEN game-invalid (bounds mismatch retail in 846/857 regions

## M.6 getTriangles' documented null is dereferenced by three of its five callers, on the live map-editing path

`src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/GeoBoxOps.java:79` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `int[] tris = model.getTriangles(g.meshIndex);` guarded only by `if (!g.posOk) continue;` at line 75, then dereferenced at line 89 `for (int t = 0; t + 2 < tris.length; t += 3)`. Same at duplicate (142/151) and delete (200/208), and at MapPrefab.java:117/127. posOk does NOT imply the index buffer was found: BchMapModel.geometry():448 `Integer es = idxElem.get(subCmdPtr + 0x10); if (es != null) {...}` leaves idxCount at 0 when the buffer is not located, line 464 then falls back to `byExtent`, and posOk (468-470) only asks about the POSITION attribute — while getTriangles returns null on exactly that miss (`if (elemSize < 0) { return null; }`, line 568). MapModelObj.export.java:64 handles it (`if (pos == null || tris == null) { skipped.add(...); continue; }`); the three write paths do not.

**What the user gets.** A mesh whose index buffer this reader cannot locate turns Move/Duplicate/Delete selection and Copy selection as prefab into a NullPointerException — GeoEditForm.java:472-478 and :189 are the buttons. The user gets a stack trace or a silent dead button on some regions and not others, with no way to tell which.

**Fix.** Change the shape so it cannot be dropped: return an empty int[] and record the mesh as undecodable in MeshGeom (a `trisOk` beside `posOk`), or make posOk require the index buffer, so "the mesh is editable" is one answer instead of two that can disagree.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed, and BchMapModel itself proves the condition is treated as reachable. `getTriangles` is documented "or null if the mesh's index buffer cannot be located" and returns null at src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/BchMapModel.java:568 (`if (elemSize < 0) { return null; }`) when no entry in `idxBuffers` has `ib[1] == subCmdPtr + 0x10`. `posOk` cannot imply otherwise: geometry() line 448 looks up that identical key (`idxElem.get(subCmdPtr + 0x10)`, and idxElem is built straight from idxBuffers at 395-396, so the two conditions are equivalent), and when it misses, line 464 falls back to `byExtent` (`g.vertexCount = idxCount > 0 ? Math.min(idxCount, byExtent) : byExtent;`) — so vertexCount can still be > 0 — while posOk at 468-470 only asks about the POSITION attribute (posOffset/posType/posElems/stride/vertexCount/coordSane) and never consults the index buffer. DECISIVE: the writers in the same file refuse this exact state by name — BchMapModel.java:784 and :889, `throw new IllegalStateException("mesh " + meshIndex + " index buffer not located")` — so the author models it as a real state on the write side while the reader answers null on the read side. Unguarded dereferences, all read and confirmed: GeoBoxOps.java:79+89 (query), :142+150 (duplicate), :200+207 (delete), MapPrefab.java:117+127 (extract) — each guarded only by `if (!g.posOk) continue;` (75/139/197/114). 

## M.7 Every setter on ItemData is unreachable, and each one silently truncates what it is given

`src/ctrmap/formats/pokedata/ItemData.java:115` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** setPriceRaw, setHeldEffect, setHeldArgument, setPacked, setFieldRoutine, setBattleRoutine, setSortIndex and setHealAmount are called by nothing — not the editor, not the battery (grep over src returns only their declarations). They write through `private void u8(int off, int v) { b[off] = (byte) v; }` (l.77-79) and LittleEndian.putU16, which is documented as storing "the low 16 bits" (LittleEndian.java:48), so setHeldEffect(300) stores 44. The class's own setEv refuses exactly this: "an EV delta is one signed byte; " + v + " would wrap and silently mean something else" (l.273-276). The live path does refuse — the form writes through RecordField.set (RecordEditPanel.java:206), which bounds-checks against the field's width (RecordField.java:144-147).

**What the user gets.** Eight public doors into a 36-byte record that is poked in place into the user's ITEM_DATA archive, each of which accepts an out-of-range value and stores a different one. The next caller to reach for the obvious ItemData.setHeldEffect gets silent wrap in a class that elsewhere argues at length against it, and there is no compile error or test to stop them.

**Fix.** Delete them — the editor does not use them and the schema path already refuses out-of-range — or give each the same range refusal setEv carries. Deleting is the refusal: a door that cannot be opened wrongly is better than one that reports its own misuse.

**Verified.** Every claimed line checks out and I could not refute it. (2) The sibling refusal is verbatim at ItemData.java:273-276: `throw new IllegalArgumentException("an EV delta is one signed byte; " + v + " would wrap and silently mean something else")`. (3) Unreached: `grep -rn "setPriceRaw|setHeldEffect|setHeldArgument|setPacked|setFieldRoutine|setBattleRoutine|setSortIndex|setHealAmount" src/` returns only the eight declarations in ItemData.java; a repo-wide grep matches nothing but build/classes/ctrmap/formats/pokedata/ItemData.class. setEv (269) and toBytes() are equally uncalled — toBytes()'s only caller is the round-trip check at ItemDataTest.java:107 — so the whole mutable half of the class is dead; production reaches ItemData only through getters (ItemTable.java:185-187, ItemEditDialog.java:638). (4) The live path does refuse, as claimed: RecordEditPanel.java:206 `f.set(record, value)` into RecordField.java:142-147, which throws "holds min..max; ... would wrap and silently mean something else". (5) No suite refu

## M.8 Each registry record closes the caller's stream when it finishes reading or writing itself

`src/ctrmap/formats/propdata/ADPropRegistry.java:159` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** ADPropRegistryEntry(LittleEndianDataInputStream dis) ends with `dis.read(u2); dis.close();` and write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos) ends with `dos.write(u2); dos.close();` - both close a stream owned by the enclosing loop, which then reads or writes the next record through it. It only works because the streams are Byte{Array}Input/OutputStream, whose close() is a no-op. `dis.read(u2)` also drops the count for the 0x24 unknown bytes.

**What the user gets.** The loop in ADPropRegistry's constructor and in write() is one stream-type change away from reading exactly one entry and writing exactly one entry, with no error - and the class is already called with hand-built streams from PropEditForm:474, PropEditForm:1121 and TilePainterForm:961. A short read on u2 silently zeroes bytes that are written straight back into the area.

**Fix.** Delete both close() calls - a record does not own the stream it is handed - and use readFully for u2.

**Verified.** The lines are exactly as claimed and I read them. ADPropRegistry.java:158-159 ends the per-record constructor with `dis.read(u2); dis.close();`, and ADPropRegistry.java:198-199 ends `write(LittleEndianDataOutputStream dos)` with `dos.write(u2); dos.close();`. Both streams belong to the enclosing loop, not the record: ADPropRegistry.java:72-73 `for (int i = 0; i < numEntries; i++) { ADPropRegistryEntry entry = new ADPropRegistryEntry(dis);` and ADPropRegistry.java:117 `e.getValue().write(dos);` keep reading/writing the next record through a stream the previous record already closed. LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:87 and LittleEndianDataOutputStream.java:51 forward close() straight to the wrapped DataInput/OutputStream, so nothing intercepts it; the loop survives only because every construction site wraps a ByteArrayInputStream/ByteArrayOutputStream, whose close() is a documented no-op (ADPropRegistry.java:69, ADPropRegistry.java:111, PropEditForm.java:474, PropEditForm.java:1121, TilePainterForm.java:961-962). It is an outlier, not a house convention: the sibling record writers CameraData.java:130/141/153 and GRCollisionMesh.java:39 never close the handed stream. The dropped-count half is also real and is the live silent-failure shape: `dis.read(u2)` at ADPropRegistry.java:158 throws away the return for a fixed 0x24 field, while this same tree already carries the refusal for i

## M.9 EditorTileset constructs successfully out of a file it could not read

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/EditorTileset.java:19` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `public EditorTileset(File f) { try { load(new FileInputStream(f)); } catch (FileNotFoundException ex) { Logger...log(SEVERE, null, ex); } }` - the object is handed back with `tiles` null. load() (:31) also `return`s on a wrong magic leaving tiles null, assigns `tiles = new TileTemplate[length]` (:39) BEFORE filling it, and does an unchecked `dis.read(strbuf)` (:43). Every accessor then dereferences it: `for (int i = 0; i < tiles.length; i++) { if (tiles[i].binary == binary)` (:57, and :66, :82). Workspace.java:85-86 is forced to probe the field - `if (ts.tiles != null) { //if yes, the loader failed, wrong magic most likely` - a comment that states the opposite of what the code tests.

**What the user gets.** A tileset truncated mid-file passes Workspace's `tiles != null` check with a half-filled array, so "The tileset is corrupt. Restoring defaults." never fires; the first tile the user clicks NPEs inside getSimpleColor/getTemplate with nothing pointing at the tileset file. A missing file gets a stack trace on a console the user does not have and then the same NPE.

**Fix.** Make the failure impossible to carry: a static `EditorTileset read(...)` that THROWS on a missing file, a wrong magic or a short record, assigning `tiles` only once every template is built. Then Workspace cannot hold an unusable tileset and its field probe (and its inverted comment) deletes itself.

**Verified.** Code is as quoted. EditorTileset.java:19-25 logs FileNotFoundException and hands back an object with `tiles` null; :35-37 returns on wrong magic; :39 `tiles = new TileTemplate[length];` allocates before the fill loop; :43 `dis.read(strbuf);` is unchecked; :51 `catch (IOException ex)` logs and exits the loop; :57/:66/:82 dereference `tiles[i]` unguarded. PARTLY REFUTED: the missing-file and wrong-magic harms do NOT reach a user, because the single caller guards them - Workspace.java:84 `if (f.exists())` before constructing and :86 `if (ts.tiles != null)` after, falling through to Ui.error("The tileset is corrupt. Restoring defaults.") at :94; the claimed "missing file ... then the same NPE" is wrong. WHAT SURVIVES is the partial-fill case that check cannot see: a stream dying after the name-length byte leaves a non-null array with null entries, passes `tiles != null`, and NPEs not on the first click but while the UI is built - TileEditForm.java:136-138 `for (int i = 0; i < tileset.tiles.length; i++) { TileTemplate tile = tileset.tiles[i]; models[tile.cat1 * 4 + tile.cat2].addElement(tile.name); }` - with the corrupt-tileset dialog two lines above never firing. The unchecked read at :43 also bites the default path: ResourceAccess.java:24 returns a classloader/JAR stream where a short read(byte[]) is legal, and a desynchronised parse yields tiles with wrong `binary` values, i.e. w

## M.10 A number that will not parse is silently written as 0 into coordinates, rotations, scales and collision vertices

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/Forms.java:34` - **api-trap** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `public static float getFloatFromDocument(JFormattedTextField docOwner)` answers `return 0f;` for an empty document, for "-", and in `catch (BadLocationException | NumberFormatException ex) { return 0f; }`. Its 18 call sites write straight into game data on every keystroke: CollEditPanel:567 `coll.meshes[selectedMesh].tris.get(selectedTri).setX(vertex, Forms.getFloatFromDocument(source)); coll.modified = true;` and PropEditForm:783-791 which fills prop2.x/y/z, rotateX/Y/Z and scaleX/Y/Z the same way.

**What the user gets.** Clearing a field or leaving a half-typed value ("1e", "12," on a layout the replace(',','.') does not cover) silently sets that vertex or prop field to 0 and marks the file modified. A collision vertex snaps to the region origin - a triangle stretched across the map - and a prop's scale of 0 makes it degenerate; both are then written to the workspace and deployed. Nothing is shown, and neither form has an undo.

**Fix.** Make the failure unrepresentable at the point of action: return a Float (or throw) so a caller must decide what an unreadable field means, and have the edit paths skip the write and mark the field invalid rather than committing 0. The formatter can also be made to refuse a commit, so the document can never be in a state this function has to invent a value for.

**Verified.** Forms.java:34-45 is exactly as claimed: `if (val.length() > 0 && !val.equals("-")) { return Float.valueOf(val); } else { return 0f; }` plus `catch (BadLocationException | NumberFormatException ex) { return 0f; }`. The 18 production call sites are real and unguarded. CollEditPanel.java:565-583 (adjustX/Y/Z) guards only `if (selectedTri != -1)` before `tris.get(selectedTri).setX(vertex, Forms.getFloatFromDocument(source)); coll.modified = true;`, and these are per-keystroke DocumentListeners on all nine vertex fields (CollEditPanel.java:61-190, insertUpdate AND removeUpdate). PropEditForm.java:783-791 fills a fresh GRProp's x/y/z, rotateX/Y/Z and scaleX/Y/Z the same way in saveProp(). Nothing stops bad text entering the document: the fields are `new NumberFormatter(new DecimalFormat("#0.00"))` (CollEditPanel.java:355-387) with only setValueClass(Float.class) applied (PropEditForm.java:700-704), and `setAllowsInvalid`, `InputVerifier` and `setFocusLostBehavior` have ZERO occurrences anywhere under src/, so Swing's default allowsInvalid=true stands. It reaches disk with no prompt: CtrmapMainframe.java:279-282 puts `mCollEditPanel.store()` in the save chain, and CollEditPanel.java:214-218 is `if (files.get(i).modified) { files.get(i).write(); }` with no askToKeep at all; Save is a window-scoped accelerator (CtrmapMainframe.java:866, VK_S), so Ctrl+S fires while the malformed field s

## M.11 A blank matrix builds its default camera boundary and throws it away

`src/ctrmap/formats/mapmatrix/MapMatrix.java:112` - **correctness** - effort: small

**What the code does.** The create constructor ends: `MatrixCameraBoundaries mcb = new MatrixCameraBoundaries(); mcb.isRepeal = 0; mcb.north = -20; mcb.south = 740; mcb.west = -20; mcb.east = 740; }` - mcb is never added to cambounds and the constructor returns. assembleCamData then writes `writeInt(cambounds.size())` = 0.

**What the user gets.** Builder.java:498 creates a new map matrix this way and calls tmp.write() immediately, so the new matrix ships with a camera-boundary subfile of four zero bytes instead of the -20..740 bounds the code plainly intends. Every value that was computed here is discarded silently; the user gets a matrix whose camera is unbounded and no indication anything was meant to be there.

**Fix.** `cambounds.add(mcb);`. A guard that builds a blank matrix and asserts one boundary with those five values refuses it.

## M.13 The zone list and the whole zone-property form are mutated from a SwingWorker background thread

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZoneLoadingPanel.java:205` - **correctness** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `protected Object doInBackground()` (line 205) does `zoneList.setSelectedIndex(-1); zoneList.removeAllItems(); tmg.setSelectedIndex(-1); tmg.removeAllItems();` (213-216), `btnAddZone.setEnabled(...)/setToolTipText(...)` (223-230), 536 x `zoneList.addItem(name); tmg.addItem(name);` (259-260) and `type.setModel(...)/weather.setModel(...)` (264, 302). The second worker's doInBackground (1272-1295) calls `loadZone(z)` - which writes forty-odd widget values - and `zoneEditors.show(z)`. Nothing in the file calls SwingUtilities.invokeLater/invokeAndWait, and the EDT is not idle meanwhile: `progress.showDialog()` runs an APPLICATION_MODAL dialog (LoadingDialog.java:65) while the worker mutates the models.

**What the user gets.** Combo-box model mutation races the EDT's painting of the same models. The town-map-group combo is visible on the Zone tab and receives 536 addItem calls off-thread; the symptom is an intermittent IndexOutOfBounds or garbled/stale dropdown during a workspace load or zone switch, on the user's machine only - no headless suite paints, so the battery cannot see it. loadZone() itself is called from both threads (store() on the EDT at 481, the worker at 1287).

**Fix.** Keep only the file reads in doInBackground and publish the parsed zones; build the list, the two models, the button state and the property form in process()/done() on the EDT. The refusal that closes the class is an assert/guard at the top of the UI-mutating helpers (`if (!SwingUtilities.isEventDispatchThread()) throw ...`) so a future call from a worker fails loudly the first time instead of intermittently.

**Verified.** Verified: see evidence field.

## M.14 One unsynchronised thread per list keystroke, all drawing into the same preview panel

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZonePreviewPane.java:138` - **correctness** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** preview() runs `final int mine = ++seq[0];` on the EDT and starts `new Thread(...)` per call; the worker reads `seq[0]` at line 154 and again in say() at line 202. `private final int[] seq = {0};` is a plain int array - no volatile, no lock - written by the EDT and read by every worker, so the newest-wins rule rests on a value with no visibility guarantee. Worse, the check at 154 is followed by draw() at 159, which calls the shared mapView().loadRegions(...): a newer request can pass its own check and enter loadRegions on the SAME TileMapPanel while an older one is still inside it. ZoneBrowserPane calls preview() on every list selection change (ZoneBrowserPane.java:116), so holding an arrow key starts a thread per row, each decoding a zone's BCH textures.

**What the user gets.** Arrowing through the zone list can show a map assembled from two zones, or throw on the panel's half-built arrays, and it spawns unbounded threads each doing a full archive read and texture decode - the fan-spinning pause the owner would feel as "the browser hangs when I scroll".

**Fix.** One worker: a single-thread executor whose pending task is replaced, or serialise draw() on the panel, and make the sequence an AtomicInteger so the newest-wins rule is actually a rule. Nothing here needs more than one reader at a time.

**Verified.** I tried to refute it and could not - the source says exactly what was claimed. ZonePreviewPane.java:47 `private final int[] seq = {0};` is a plain int array (grep for synchronized/volatile/Atomic in that file returns ONE hit, `private synchronized TileMapPanel mapView()` at :228, which guards only the lazy construction of the panel, not the drawing). ZonePreviewPane.java:138 `final int mine = ++seq[0];` runs on the EDT, and :140 `Thread t = new Thread(...)` starts a fresh daemon thread per call, so later EDT increments have no happens-before edge to an already-running worker. The decisive half is not the visibility though, it is check-then-act: :154 `if (mine != seq[0]) { free(zone); return; }` is followed at :159 by `draw(zone, reading)` -> :193 `mapView().loadRegions(...)`, and TileMapPanel.loadRegions (TileMapPanel.java:521) is NOT synchronized and assigns the panel's own shared fields - :526 `mm = matrix;` :527-531 `width/height/tilemaps = new Tilemap[mm.width][mm.height]/models/tallgrass/colls` then `collision.unload()` - and its loop at :533 re-reads the field each iteration (`for (int i = 0; i < mm.height; i++)` ... `mm.ids.get(j, i)`, `mm.regions.get(j, i)`, writing `tilemaps[j][i]`). Two workers that both pass their :154 check enter that body on the SAME TileMapPanel instance (mapView() returns the one cached `map`), so a newer request replacing `mm` and the arrays mid

Line 561 additionally reads the JComboBox `garc` (:539) off the EDT. The panel is live UI: CtrmapMainframe.java:558 `mBuilder = new Builder();` and :1104 `return buildExtrasTab(new ExtrasPanel(loadedZone), mBuilder);`, and the user must be on that tab to press the button. The modal progress dialog does not serialise it - LoadingDialog.java:65 `ret.dlg.setModalityType(Dialog.ModalityType.APPLICATION_MODAL);` with `worker.execute(); dlg.showDialog();` (Builder.java:566-567) leaves the EDT in a nested pump still painting the frame behind the dialog while the worker rewrites both models. done() (:539) is on the EDT, already calls get() (:543), and is where the two refresh calls belong. This is not the house pattern: the other 15 doInBackground bodies in src do model-f

## M.17 The byte-stream primitives live in the application package, welding 26 of 45 packages into one dependency cycle

`src/ctrmap/LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:1` - **coupling** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:1 and LittleEndianDataOutputStream.java declare `package ctrmap;` - the same package as Workspace, CtrmapMainframe and Ui. I parsed the import graph over all 264 production files: 45 packages, 20 mutually dependent package pairs, and one strongly connected component of 26 packages. Eight of those 20 pairs (ctrmap <-> ctrmap.formats, .containers, .garc, .h3d, .mapmatrix, .text, .tilemap, .zone) exist ONLY because of these two classes: of the 43 `import ctrmap.<TopLevelClass>` lines inside ctrmap.formats, 41 are one of the two streams, spread over 24 format classes (Triangle, CameraCoordinates/Data/DataFile, AbstractGamefreakContainer, GARC, GRCollisionBounds/File/Mesh, H3DModelNameGet, RandomAccessBAIS, StringUtils, MapMatrix, MatrixCameraBoundaries, NPCRegistry, ADPropRegistry, GRProp, GRPropData, GFLPawnScript, PawnPrefixEntry, TextFile, Tilemap, ZoneEntities, ZoneHeader). ctrmap.util already exists for exactly this and says so - Bytes.java:22-24: "What is here needs no game, no workspace, no window and no display. That is the whole rule, and it is why the format layer may use this class freely."

**What the user gets.** The format layer cannot be compiled, read, or reused without the package that holds the application's global, its window and its dialogs, so "the format layer stands below everything" can never become a flat rule. It is why SourceSeamTest's format rule has to be a member-by-member allowlist (PURE_UTILS / PURE_UTILS_EDGES) rather than "no class under ctrmap.formats names ctrmap.* at all" - and an allowlist is the shape a new member slips into.

**Fix.** Move both stream classes to ctrmap.util beside Bytes (an import rewrite in 25 files, no behaviour change). Eight of the 20 package cycles vanish and SourceSeamTest's format rule can become the flat, allowlist-free predicate.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED on the structural fact, with two corrections to the claimed harm. Verified in source: src/ctrmap/LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:1 and src/ctrmap/LittleEndianDataOutputStream.java:1 both read `package ctrmap;` — the same package as src/ctrmap/Workspace.java, src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java and src/ctrmap/Ui.java. The import counts are exact, not approximate: `grep -rh "^import ctrmap\.[A-Z]" src/ctrmap/formats/` yields 22 LittleEndianDataInputStream + 19 LittleEndianDataOutputStream + 2 ctrmap.Workspace = 43, i.e. 41 of 43, spread over exactly 24 files (CameraCoordinates/Data/DataFile, AbstractGamefreakContainer, GARC, GRCollisionBounds/File/Mesh, H3DModelNameGet, RandomAccessBAIS, StringUtils, MapMatrix, MatrixCameraBoundaries, NPCRegistry, ADPropRegistry, GRProp, GRPropData, GFLPawnScript, PawnPrefixEntry, TextFile, Tilemap, Triangle, ZoneEntities, ZoneHeader) — the census's list is right file-for-file. I re-ran the SCC myself over the 264 production files (tests excluded). With the two stream classes: largest SCC = 28 packages, 21 mutually dependent pairs. Deleting ONLY those two import edges and changing nothing else: largest SCC = 9, mutual pairs = 13 — and the 8 pairs that vanish are precisely the ones named (ctrmap <-> ctrmap.formats, .containers, .garc, .h3d, .mapmatrix, .text, .tilemap, .zone). So the claim understates rather than overstates: two files hold 

## M.18 The tile and matrix cursors are process-wide public statics cleared by two half-clearing methods; the hover half survives a zone load

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/Selector.java:10` - **coupling** - effort: large

**What the code does.** Selector.java:10-15 holds 5 public static mutable fields (hilightTileX/Y, selTileX/Y, selecting) read by 12 production classes including 8 tools; MatrixSelector.java:23-29 holds 6 more. Clearing is split: `deselect()` (Selector.java:68-72) clears only the hover, `unfocus()` (80-86) clears only the pick, with 14 call sites between them. TileMapPanel.loadMatrix (482) calls `Selector.unfocus(this)` and NOT deselect, so after a zone load hilightTileX/Y still name a tile of the replaced map, and loadMatrix does its work in a SwingWorker (483-500) so the map changes under a stationary pointer. Every guard in the tree tests `hilightTileX == -1` (TilemapPanelInputManager.java:90, FillTool.java:57, NPCTool.java:84, TriggerTool.java:116, WarpTool.java:132) - a stale non-(-1) value passes all of them. TileMapPanel.getRegionForTile (834-839) is `return tilemaps[x / 40][y / 40];` with no bounds check. The shape has already produced two recorded user-visible defects: TileEditForm.java:337 ("THE CURSOR OUTLIVES THE MAP IT WAS SET ON... nothing resets them when a zone is loaded") and MatrixEditForm.java:64-72 ("MatrixSelector.unfocus() ... never had a caller ... so selRegionX/selRegionY survived every zone load", drawing the picked-cell rectangle off the grid). MatrixSelector.java:17-19 states the debt itself: "Still static, and that is the remaining problem rather than a decision: four public mutable statics mean one cursor for the whole program." OUTSTANDING.md's Open list is empty, so nothing tracks it and no suite refuses it.

**What the user gets.** Load a wide zone, pick a tile, then load a smaller zone without moving the mouse. A left mouse-down now runs FillTool.onTileMouseDown (FillTool.java:89-91) `originX = Selector.hilightTileX;` and anchors the fill rectangle at a tile of the previous map - filling the user's new map from a corner they never clicked. A press that reaches Selector.acqCurTile (Selector.java:43, via EditTool.java:28 / SetTool.java:32 / FillTool.java:83) calls getRegionForTile with the stale coordinates and throws ArrayIndexOutOfBounds on the event thread when the cell is off the new map, leaving the form half-updated with a stack trace on stderr.

**Fix.** The cursor belongs to the panel it points into: make hilight/sel instance fields of TileMapPanel (and of the matrix panel), so loadMatrix cannot leave one behind and there is one clear() instead of two half-clears spread over 14 call sites. Until then the minimum is that loadMatrix clears both halves and getRegionForTile refuses an out-of-range tile instead of indexing.

**Verified.** Every cited line says what the census says, and I read the callers. (1) src/ctrmap/humaninterface/Selector.java:10-15 really is five public static mutable fields, and the clear is really split: deselect() (68-72) sets only hilightTileX/Y = -1, unfocus() (80-86) sets only selTileX/Y = -1. (2) src/ctrmap/humaninterface/TileMapPanel.java:482 is `Selector.unfocus(this);` with no deselect, and its own comment at 477-481 states the reason the OTHER half was cleared - "picking a tile on a large zone then loading a smaller one threw on the EDT out of getRegionForTile" - which is the identical coordinate pair with the identical readers. (3) The hover half has exactly ONE resetter in the whole tree: `grep -rn "Selector.deselect"` returns only TilemapPanelInputManager.java:83, inside moveSelector's else branch (pointer inside the panel but off the scaled image). TileMapPanel.java:1021/1031 set the panel's preferred size to the image's size, so in the common case panel == image and that else branch never runs while the pointer is inside - the hover coordinate simply survives the pointer leaving, and Selector.select:27 `if (map.loaded)` means motion while nothing is loaded does not clear it either. (4) getRegionForTile (TileMapPanel.java:834-838) is `if (tilemaps == null) return null; return tilemaps[x / 40][y / 40];` - null-safe, not bounds-safe; TileMapPanelStateTest.java:957 states this 

## M.19 A failed extraction leaves a zero-byte staged file that every later read, and the pack, treat as the entry

`src/ctrmap/WorkspaceSession.java:505` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** getWorkspaceFile: `OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(wsFile);` is opened BEFORE the bytes exist, then `byte[] b = getArchive(arc).getDecompressedEntry(fileNum); if (b == null) { os.close(); return null; }` and the surrounding `catch (IOException ex) { Logger...log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex); }` falls through to `return wsFile;`. getDecompressedEntry itself answers null on any IOException (GARC.java:707 logs and returns null). The next call re-enters with `if (!wsFile.exists() && ...)` - which is now false - so the empty/partial file is handed back as the extracted entry forever.

**What the user gets.** One transient read failure (truncated archive, a locked file, an entry past entries.size()) permanently poisons the workspace with a 0-byte region/zone/area. Nothing downstream distinguishes it: GeometryForker.java:250-255 null-checks and then does `Files.write(out, Files.readAllBytes(srcRegionFile))` + addPersist, so the empty file is copied into a new region and packed into the user's FieldData. The user sees a zone with no ground and no message was ever shown.

**Fix.** Write to a temp name and rename only after the bytes are in hand; delete the partial on any failure; and throw instead of logging, so the caller cannot mistake 'could not extract' for 'this entry is empty'. The pattern already exists in the same tree (GARC.writeRepacked stages `<name>_new` then Files.move).

**Verified.** CONFIRMED as written, with two of the claimed consequences overstated. The code is verbatim what was claimed: src/ctrmap/WorkspaceSession.java:504 gates on `if (!wsFile.exists() && getArchive(arc).length > fileNum)`, :506 opens `OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(wsFile);` BEFORE any bytes exist, :507-511 `byte[] b = getArchive(arc).getDecompressedEntry(fileNum); if (b == null) { os.close(); return null; }` — leaving the just-created 0-byte file on disk — and :515-517 `catch (IOException ex) { Logger...log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex); }` falls straight through to :519 `return wsFile;`, so a write that threw half-way, or an open that never happened, is handed back as the extracted entry. Nothing deletes the stub, and :504's `exists()` gate means every later call in that session returns it without re-extracting. REACHABLE, measured in this repo: GARC.getDecompressedEntry answers null on any IOException (src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:696-698 `catch (IOException ex) { Logger...; return null; }`), and GARC.parse (GARC.java:55-129) uses RandomAccessFile.read, which does NOT throw on a short read, so a truncated/half-copied archive parses with `length` = the declared count and only faults inside readFully at extraction time. The project's own suite proves that exact behaviour for the identical sibling reader: src/ctrmap/test

## M.20 An append that fails at the text fork leaves the workspace half-forked and then refuses the retry

`src/ctrmap/ZoneAppender.java:277` - **data-integrity** - effort: large

**What the code does.** appendZones runs `GeometryForker.forkAppendedZones(...)`, then `AreaForker.forkAppendedAreas(p.newZos, p.master, created)`, then `TextForker.forkAppendedTexts(...)`, and only afterwards writes the zone containers and master (lines 308-319). The first two WRITE as they go - AreaForker.java:313 `Files.write(adOut.toPath(), plan.newAdBytes); Workspace.addPersist(adOut);`, the filler registries at 316-321, and the grown per-area table at 337 - while TextForker.java:99-102 throws for a donor whose story text cannot be read.

**What the user gets.** The user is told the append failed while the workspace holds staged regions, areas, NPC registries and a grown engine per-area table that no zone names; the next Pack Workspace writes all of it into the game. The retry is then refused by AreaForker.java:288 ("An area fork is already staged and not yet packed") until they pack the wreckage. The method's own comment promises the opposite: the budget is checked up front "rather than running out half way and leaving two of four new zones private".

**Fix.** Plan all three forks as bytes before anything touches the filesystem - buildMultiAppendPayloads already proves the shape for the zone payloads - and write once at the end, or stage into a directory that is committed only on success.

**Verified.** Confirmed on the source. Order in ZoneAppender.appendZones: GeometryForker.forkAppendedZones at ZoneAppender.java:277, AreaForker.forkAppendedAreas at :294, TextForker.forkAppendedTexts at :302, and only then the zone containers/master/EN at :310-319. The first two stage to disk as they go: GeometryForker.java:254-255 `Files.write(out.toPath(), Files.readAllBytes(srcRegionFile.toPath())); Workspace.addPersist(out);` plus the matrix at :259-260, and AreaForker.java:313-315 `Files.write(adOut.toPath(), plan.newAdBytes); Workspace.addPersist(adOut); registerPendingArea(...)`, the NPC filler registries at :318-320, the new registry at :323-325 and the grown per-area table at :337-338. TextForker then throws mid-run at TextForker.java:99-102 `if (src == null || !src.isFile()) throw new IOException("Story text file " + oldText + " could not be read out of the workspace, so zone ... cannot be given a copy of it.")`. That branch is reachable, not decorative: WorkspaceSession.getWorkspaceFile (WorkspaceSession.java:501-519) returns a File that does not exist when the entry is past the archive, and swallows a failed extraction with `Logger...log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex)` at :513-515 before returning the same non-existent File - the identical condition NPCEditForm.java:733-735 raises as "story text file N could not be extracted from the STORYTEXT archive". TextForker can also throw at :88 

## M.21 GARC.parse reads the archive magic and drops it, so a file that is not an archive is accepted as one

`src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:70` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `String garcMagic = new String(strbuf);` - and nothing in production ever compares it: `grep -rn 'CRAG' src` matches only HandedGameTest.java:1655. endian, version, sectionCount, decompressedLength and compressedLength are read and dropped the same way. The sibling container class refuses on exactly this (AbstractGamefreakContainer.verify(), 'its first two bytes are 0x... and this container is 0x...'), with a javadoc explaining that an unverified open 'produced an object the rest of the editor treated as a zone or an area'.

**What the user gets.** One capability - 'decide whether this file is the container I think it is' - with two implementations, and only one of them can say no. A workspace pointed at a folder whose archive slot holds something else (a wrong dump layout, an already-extracted file) gets a GARC object built from whatever bytes 4..8 happen to say, and packDirectory then REPLACES that file with a rebuilt archive. ModDeployer.garcContentsEqual (line 312) is written in the belief that `new GARC(a)` throws for a non-archive.

**Fix.** Refuse in parse() when the magic is not CRAG, naming the file and the bytes found, the way verify() already does for the mini-containers.

THE LINE IS THERE. src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:70 `String garcMagic = new String(strbuf);` — assigned and never read; same for endian (:72), version (:73), decompressedLength (:76), compressedLength (:77). `grep -rn 'CRAG' src` matches only src/ctrmap/tests/HandedGameTest.java:1655 (a test WRITING the magic). Nothing in production compares it. THE ACCEPT PATH IS NOT SPECULATIVE — I MEASURED IT. I replayed parse() (GARC.java:56-139) byte-for-byte, with Java's RandomAccessFile semantics (seek past EOF legal, read(byte[]) returns -1 without throwing, readInt/readShort throw EOFException), over 1,926 real files >= 0x1C bytes under the repo: 1,849 refused, 5 threw on a bad length, and 72 (3.7%) parsed clean with no exception at all. Concretely, dist/app/CTRMap-F5/CTRMap-F5.exe (magic "MZ\x90\x00"): garcLength=3, dataOffset=184, fatoLength=1024, fatoEntries=-256 -> the constructor returns an object with `length` == -256 and `getEntryCount()` == 0. dist/CTRMap-F5-1.0.2-portable.zip ("PK\x03\x04") returns length == 0. ## M.22 A short Maison class-list record silently becomes a shorter class list, and the shorter count is written back

`src/ctrmap/formats/maison/MaisonClassList.java:40` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** read() takes `int count = u16(rec, 2)` and then loops `for (int i = 0; i < count && 4 + i * 2 + 1 < rec.length; i++)` — when the record is shorter than its own declared count the loop just stops, and nothing records that count and setIndices.size() disagree. write() then emits `putU16(out, 2, setIndices.size())` (l.57): the declared count is replaced by however many the reader managed to take.

**What the user gets.** This is the camera-table defect (OUTSTANDING item J, closed 2026-09-13) in another table: a record declaring more entries than it holds is read as a short record and saved as the truth. A trainer class that drew from 66 opponent sets is rewritten as drawing from the handful that survived the read, and the Battle Maison quietly stops using the rest — with the facility opponent editor reporting a successful save. The a/1/8/3 table stores fixed 136-byte entries, so a single truncated or odd-length entry is enough.

**Fix.** Refuse: a record whose declared count does not fit its length is not a class list, and read() should say so with the class tag, the count and the length, the way MaisonSet.read already refuses a short record (MaisonSet.java:104-106). A reader that cannot hand back what the record declared must not hand back something shorter that looks identical.

**Verified.** The source says exactly what the census claimed, and I could not find a caller, suite or upstream check that stops it. MaisonClassList.read refuses a header it cannot read — `if (rec == null || rec.length < 4) throw new IllegalArgumentException("class-list record too short")` (src/ctrmap/formats/maison/MaisonClassList.java:33-35) — but then tolerates a body it cannot read: `for (int i = 0; i < count && 4 + i * 2 + 1 < rec.length; i++)` (line 40) stops early with no signal, and nothing on the object records that `count` (line 39) and `setIndices.size()` disagree. write() then launders the disagreement: `putU16(out, 2, setIndices.size())` (line 57) with `int len = Math.max(tight, sourceLength)` (line 54) re-emits a record of the SAME truncated length whose declared count now matches the short read, and the tail is overwritten with 0xFFFF by the padding loop (lines 61-63) — the original bytes and the original count are both gone, and the record is now self-consistent so nothing can detect the loss afterwards. The write path is live: MaisonClassListDialog.ListModel.loadTable reads each row from a loose workspace file (src/ctrmap/humaninterface/MaisonClassListDialog.java:186-188) and save() unconditionally writes EVERY row back with `fos.write(lists[i].write())` + `Workspace.addPersist(f)` (lines 210-217), so one short row is committed to the workspace on any save of the table, with

## M.23 A registry that only half-read is written back as the whole registry

`src/ctrmap/formats/npcreg/NPCRegistry.java:207` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** The constructor fills `entries` entry by entry and, on an IOException part-way, keeps whatever it got (l.55-63); nothing records that the read ended early. store() then does `new FileOutputStream(f)` — which truncates — and writes only what is in the map (l.207-214), in `HashMap` iteration order (`entries` is declared `new HashMap<>()` at l.36), not the order the file held. The format has no length field and no index ("this struct does not describe length in any way", l.54), so nothing downstream can tell a short registry from a complete one.

**What the user gets.** Two ways to lose game data in one method: a registry that read partially is saved with the missing NPC model entries gone for good, and even a clean load is rewritten in a different record order than the game shipped — a file this editor does not fully understand, reordered on every save that touches it.

**Fix.** Record that the read completed and make store() refuse a registry that did not — a writer must not be able to overwrite a file from a parse it knows failed. Use a LinkedHashMap so the file order the reader saw is the order the writer emits.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED IN PART - the reorder half is real and measured; the partial-read half is refuted. ## M.24 The one in-place byte-poker never asks the staleness question its own archive class answers

`src/ctrmap/formats/pokedata/ItemTable.java:271` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** writeRecord checks length, id range, compression and file length, then `int off = garc.getEntryFileOffset(id);` and `raf.seek(off); raf.write(rec);` on the live game archive. GARC.isStale() (line 268) exists precisely for 'every offset this instance holds now points at the wrong place' and packDirectory calls rereadIfStale() before using its table; writeRecord never does. The ItemTable instance holds its GARC for the life of the editing session (ItemTable.java:152, reload() is only called from open()).

**What the user gets.** Anything that rewrites a/1/9/7 while the item editor is open - Vault.restoreOne, which is designed for exactly 'put one archive back', or a second tool - moves the records. The next Save then pokes 36 bytes at the old offset, landing in the middle of a different record, and the read-back check passes because it reads back the same wrong place. The archive is not in MODDABLE, so the pristine snapshot cannot show what it used to hold.

**Fix.** Refuse when garc.isStale() (or re-read and recompute the offset) before the seek - the check is one call and the class already publishes it.

**Verified.** Confirmed at ItemTable.java:271 `int off = garc.getEntryFileOffset(id);` followed by ItemTable.java:279-281 `raf.seek(off); raf.write(rec);`. Every per-entry fact writeRecord validates is read from the cached GARC parse (ItemTable.java:262 isEntryCompressed, :266 getEntryStoredLength, :271 getEntryFileOffset); the only live-file fact is garcFile.length() at :272, which catches truncation but not a same-or-longer file with a different layout, and the read-back at :289-292 seeks the SAME off so it confirms the wrong place. GARC.isStale() exists at GARC.java:241 with a javadoc naming this exact hazard ("every offset this instance holds now points at the wrong place... It is silent"), and packDirectory calls rereadIfStale() at GARC.java:288; grep over src shows ItemTable never references isStale or any re-read. The instance is long-lived: reload() (ItemTable.java:151-152, `garc = new GARC(garcFile, false)`) is called only from open() at :132, ItemEditSession captures the table once (ItemEditSession.java:63), and ItemEditDialog.java:90 is `public static JComponent panel(...)` whose javadoc says "It was a modal window... only the frame around it" changed - OUTSTANDING.md:164 confirms the item editor now lives in the Game Data tab, so nothing bounds the session. ## M.33 Workspace settings writes four global settings before the question that can cancel the save, and the pane's close can never refuse

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/WorkspaceSettings.java:80` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** save() assigns Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH, ESPICA_PATH, TILESET_DEFAULT and TILESET_PATH (lines 80-83) and only THEN calls repointGameDir, which returns without running applySettings when the user declines the backup (line 131) or closes/cancels the state-change warning (line 149). applySettings is what calls Workspace.validate(), reloadTileset.run() and map.updateAll(). Separately, closeRequested() returns true on every path, while its own javadoc says "It answers false when a save was refused - ... and dropping it would discard the edit silently" and FormPanel's contract (FormPanel.java:80-86) says the same; the generated code it cites says setDefaultCloseOperation(DISPOSE_ON_CLOSE), not DO_NOTHING_ON_CLOSE (line 220).

**What the user gets.** Cancelling the repoint leaves the program pointing at a new workspace directory and a new tileset while the game folder is unchanged and nothing has been validated or reloaded - a half-applied configuration the user was told nothing about. And leaving the pane after a refused save discards the edit exactly as the comment says it must not.

**Fix.** Settle the game folder first and assign the four statics only on the path that reaches applySettings (or apply them inside applySettings). Make save() answer whether it applied, and have closeRequested() return that answer, so the documented refusal exists instead of being described.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed, and I could not refute it. The path is reachable: btnSave calls save() directly (:383) and the pane exposes all four fields; it only needs the user to edit a path field and the game field in one visit and then decline. The harm is not cosmetic. Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH is read live: GeoEditForm.save() marks an edited GR file as persisted only if `gr.getOriginFile().getAbsolutePath().startsWith(new java.io.File(Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH).getAb

## M.34 "Could not clone the zone" is shown after the destination zone has already been overwritten

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZoneLoadingPanel.java:1671` - **data-integrity** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** Lines 1671-1682 are one try: `ZoneCloner.cloneIntoSlot(srcIndex, dstIndex); clearForkDecline(dstIndex); if (doFork) { ctrmap.GeometryForker.ensurePrivate(dstIndex); }` with a single `catch (Exception ex)` that says `"Could not clone the zone:\n" + ex.getMessage()` and returns. But cloneIntoSlot has already finished writing: src/ctrmap/ZoneCloner.java:49-52 does `cloneIntoFiles(src, dst, master, ...); Workspace.addPersist(dst); Workspace.addPersist(master);` - the destination ZO is overwritten and the master zone-header row patched, both staged for the next pack, before ensurePrivate is even called.

**What the user gets.** A fork that fails (GeometryForker appends to FieldData, MapMatrix and the master table - plenty to fail on) tells the user the clone did not happen. It did: the destination zone is already replaced and persisted, and because the method returns before the pack/reload, the zone list still shows the old occupant. The replacement reaches the game at the next pack, in a slot the user believes was untouched, with no reload and no second warning.

**Fix.** Make clone+fork one operation that cannot half-happen: either fork inside the cloner's own staged transaction, or catch ensurePrivate separately and report the truth - "zone N WAS replaced but did not get its own private map" - then run the same pack/reload/select tail the success path runs. The generalisable refusal is that no handler may report "could not" over a write that already persisted.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what was claimed. src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZoneLoadingPanel.java:1671-1683 is one try block: line 1672 `ZoneCloner.cloneIntoSlot(srcIndex, dstIndex);`, 1673 `clearForkDecline(dstIndex);`, 1676 `ctrmap.GeometryForker.ensurePrivate(dstIndex);`, then 1679-1682 `catch (Exception ex) { Logger...log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex); ctrmap.Ui.error(this, "Could not clone the zone:\n" + ex.getMessage(), "Clone zone"); return; }`. The write is already committed before the fork runs: src/ctrmap/ZoneCloner.java:96-97 does `Files.write(dstZo.toPath(), cloned); Files.write(masterFile.toPath(), master);` and lines 50-51 then `Workspace.addPersist(dst); Workspace.addPersist(master);` — and addPersist is durable staging, not a transaction: WorkspaceSession.java:523-525 appends the absolute path to persistPaths, which is written to ctrmap_persist.txt (line 358-364) and survives packs, so the overwritten destination ZO and the patched master row reach the GARC at the NEXT pack. There is no rollback, no finally, and the `return;` at 1682 skips both `loadEverything()` branches, so the zone list keeps naming the old occupant. The path is reachable, not hypothetical: ensurePrivate declares IOException and GeometryForker.java:688-691 always routes a fresh clone into forkGeo

## M.35 A map type or weather the dropdown tables cannot name is written back as -1 (0xFF) or as "None"

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZoneLoadingPanel.java:366` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** store() line 366 `zone.header.mapType = getTypeRaw(type.getSelectedIndex());` and 378 `zone.header.weather = getWeatherRaw(weather.getSelectedIndex());`. getTypeIndex answers `default: return -1;` (line 667) for raw 4, 5 and 10+; setSelectedIndex(-1) does NOT throw, so loadZone completes and `loaded` becomes true. getTypeRaw then answers `default: return -1;` (694) for that -1 selection, and ZoneHeader.assembleData does `dos.write(mapType)` - -1 stores as 0xFF. The -1 answer travels from "nothing is selected" to a byte on disk with no check in between.

## M.36 Save Zone writes the master zone-header table with a raw 0x38 and an unchecked row length

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/ZoneLoadingPanel.java:462` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** ZoneLoadingPanel.java:462-470 is the path that saves a user's edited zone header into the master table the game boots from: `dos.skipBytes(zoneIndex * 0x38); byte[] test = new byte[0x38]; dos.read(test); byte[] replace = zone.file.getFile(0); if (!Arrays.equals(replace, test)) { dos.seek(zoneIndex * 0x38); dos.write(replace); ... }`. The row stride is a raw literal here, a fourth spelling alongside the three named MASTER_ROW constants (GeometryForker.java:59, WorkspaceIntegrity.java:44, ZoneLimitPatch.java:49). Nothing checks that `replace` is 0x38 bytes: Arrays.equals returns false for any other length, so the branch is taken and `dos.write(replace)` writes exactly as many bytes as the subfile happens to hold, starting at zoneIndex * 0x38.

**What the user gets.** A ZO container whose subfile 0 is not exactly 0x38 bytes writes past the end of its own row into the next zone's header (or leaves the tail of its own row holding the previous occupant's bytes) in the table the game reads at boot - silently, with the dialog reporting a successful save. The same file already documents that this write is where a loose zone nearly landed in slot 0 by accident, so the write has a history of being wrong in ways nothing said.

**Fix.** Use the one MASTER_ROW constant here (finding 1's consolidation), and refuse a header subfile whose length is not the row stride before seeking - a refusal naming the zone and the two lengths, rather than a write that cannot know it is short.

**Verified.** Confirmed at the line. ZoneLoadingPanel.java:460-470 writes the master zone-header table with `dos.seek(zoneIndex * 0x38); dos.write(replace);` where `replace = zone.file.getFile(0)` and nothing checks its length or nullity. The length is data-driven, not fixed: AbstractGamefreakContainer.getFile (AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:215-240) returns `offsets[fileNum+1] - offsets[fileNum]` bytes and its javadoc says "One subfile, whole, or null"; verify() (AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:138-178) constrains only the ZO magic, offset monotonicity and in-file bounds - it never requires subfile 0 to be 0x38. Arrays.equals is false for a short array, a long array and null alike, so every one of those takes the write branch: a long subfile 0 overruns into zone+1's row, a short one leaves the tail of the row holding the previous occupant's bytes, and null is an NPE at `dos.write(replace)` that the `catch (IOException ex)` on line 471 does not catch - so the hand-written "saved, but the master zone-header table was NOT updated" report never fires and the save half-completes (the .zo is already written by then). The row stride is a raw literal here while every sibling writer of this same table names it AND bounds-checks the row first: ZoneCloner.patchMasterRow (ZoneCloner.java:128-133, throws "Master table row out of bounds"), ZoneAppender.java:355, GeometryForker.java:379, AreaForker.java:

## M.37 A vault seals "../code.bin" into its manifest, and restoring writes outside the folder it was given

`src/ctrmap/vault/Vault.java:399` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** filesToSeal adds the executable by literal relative path: `for (String exe : new String[]{"code.bin", "exefs/code.bin", "../code.bin"}) { if (f.isFile()) out.add(exe); }` - so "../code.bin" becomes a manifest KEY. sealRaw then writes `new File(filesDir, r)`, which escapes the entry's files/ folder, and restoreOne writes `new File(targetRoot, relPath)` (line 549), which escapes the target the caller chose. Updater.unzip refuses exactly this for a downloaded zip ("The package contains an unsafe path", Updater.java:661-664), so the tree already knows the rule.

**What the user gets.** Restoring a vault into anywhere other than the original dump folder - a copy, a test folder, a fresh extract - silently writes code.bin into that folder's PARENT, overwriting an unrelated executable outside the directory the user pointed at, while the confirmation said "Edits kept in your workspace are not affected" and named a file count. It also means a manifest key controls a write path, which is the shape of a path-traversal if a manifest is ever edited or damaged.

**Fix.** Keys must be inside the tree. Store the executable under a name that cannot escape (e.g. "exefs/code.bin" or a reserved "_exefs/code.bin") and record the real destination as data rather than as "..", then refuse any key containing a ".." segment on both seal and restore - the same canonical-path check unzip already makes.

**Verified.** The only vault suite is src/ctrmap/tests/VaultGuardsTest.java and it never reaches this code: all three seals use Scope.FULL_RAW (lines 92, 109, 125), which takes the collect() tree-walk branch that can never emit a `..` segment, and its fake dump puts the executable INSIDE the dump (line 286 `write(new File(dump, "code.bin"), "executable")`), so MODDABLE and the exe loop at Vault.java:399 are never exercised and nothing asserts that a restore stays inside targetRoot. OUTSTANDING.md contains no vault entry at all (grep -i vault returns nothing) and no traversal/escape item. Severity medium rather than high: the write is real and unguarded, but wrong bytes only land somewhere unintended when MODDABLE was chosen AND the dump has a sibling code.bin AND the restore target is a different folder from the one sealed; what gets written is the pristine code.bin, so a same-folder restore lands correctly.

## M.38 Vault restore truncates the user's game file before it knows it can replace it

`src/ctrmap/vault/Vault.java:616` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** pipe(): `OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(dst);` opens the LIVE game archive for truncation and streams into it; restoreOne (540-585) calls it directly on `new File(targetRoot, relPath)` and only afterwards digests the result - 'Restored X but it does not match the backup - the vault is damaged.' restoreAll (587-612) collects that string and carries on to the next file.

**What the user gets.** This is the recovery path - the thing reached for when the game is already damaged. A failure part way (the vault file unreadable, disk full, the emulator holding the archive) leaves the target archive truncated with no copy anywhere, and restoreAll keeps going, so the game folder ends as a mixture of restored, original and half-written archives. The post-hoc digest can report the damage but cannot undo it. The safe shape is in the same tree: GARC.replaceWith and GarcRebuilder both write beside the target and Files.move over it.

**Fix.** pipe into `<dst>_restore`, verify the digest there, then Files.move over the target; delete the temp and report on any failure so the original is untouched by construction.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed. src/ctrmap/vault/Vault.java:616 is `OutputStream os = new FileOutputStream(dst);` — a truncating open, no temp file, no move. restoreOne computes `File dst = new File(targetRoot, relPath);` (Vault.java:549) and hands that live path straight to pipe() at Vault.java:560 (zip scope) and :570 (raw scope); the digest check that would catch a bad result runs only afterwards at :579-585 ("Restored ... but it does not match the backup - the vault is damaged."). restoreAll at Vault.java:606-609 appends that string to `bad` and continues the loop. The safer shape is genuinely in the same tree and unused here: GARC.java:594 `Files.move(newGARC.toPath(), file.toPath(), StandardCopyOption.REPLACE_EXISTING);` and GarcRebuilder.java:101 do the same move-over-target. So a mid-stream failure (disk full, unreadable vault stream, process kill) leaves a truncated archive in the user's game folder where a write-beside-then-move would have left the original intact. TWO CORRECTIONS to the claimed harm. (1) "no copy anywhere" is false — pipe() reads FROM the vault and writes TO the game folder, so the sealed bytes survive and a retry is available; Vault.verify already proves the vault copy. (2) It is not a silent failure: VaultUi.java:166-176 reports the mixed outcome loudly — "The game folder is now part o

## M.43 The batch area fork is missing the out-of-step refusal its single-zone twin has, and overwrites an existing registry

`src/ctrmap/AreaForker.java:299` - **duplication** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** forkArea refuses first: `if (np.length > newArea + 1) { throw new IOException("The NPC registry (" + np.length + " entries) already reaches past the new area id " + newArea + "; it and AreaData ... are out of step, and an area fork would not repair that."); }` (lines 369-373). forkAppendedAreas - the path every created zone takes - has no such check; it goes straight to `for (int filler = npReaches; filler < newArea; filler++)` and then `Files.write(npOut.toPath(), plan.newNpcBytes)` at the new id. The two bodies are otherwise the same sequence written twice (stage area, fill registry gap, write registry, grow table, persist).

**What the user gets.** When NPCRegistries already reaches past the new area id - the state forkArea calls unrepairable - the batch path writes over a registry entry that is already there, which is the stranded/mis-slotted registry wreckage WorkspaceIntegrity check 4 exists to describe after the fact. One fork body, two rule sets: the fix landed on the path the user takes less often.

**Fix.** One fork body that both entry points call with a count (the batch is the general case; a single fork is count 1), so a precondition cannot exist on only one of them.

**Verified.** Verified in source. forkArea refuses the out-of-step state at src/ctrmap/AreaForker.java:369-373 ("The NPC registry (" + np.length + " entries) already reaches past the new area id " + newArea + ... "out of step, and an area fork would not repair that."). forkAppendedAreas (src/ctrmap/AreaForker.java:265-337) has no equivalent: its only pre-writes checks are requireForkSupport (271), null archives (275), the 8-bit budget (279-284), isPendingArtifact (287) and the table read (290-294), then it enters the loop at 299 with newArea = firstArea + i where firstArea = ad.length (278) - the SAME id forkArea computes at 359 - runs the filler loop `for (int filler = npReaches; filler < newArea; filler++)` (316), which is a no-op when npReaches > newArea, and writes `Files.write(npOut.toPath(), plan.newNpcBytes)` + addPersist at src/ctrmap/AreaForker.java:322-324. GARC.plan (src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:384-397) only refuses a file named past the tail, so that write REPLACES the bytes of an already-existing registry entry rather than appending. Reachable: callers are ZoneAppender.java:294 (every created zone) and GeometryForker.java:416 (the repair path for workspaces older builds damaged); grep for "Integrity" in both files returns nothing, so no caller pre-checks that AreaData and NPCRegistries are in step. Two caveats against the census's framing, neither of which refutes the asym

## M.44 The zone-header record layout is stated four ways; ZoneResource, the table written to end that, is used by one writer of four

`src/ctrmap/GeometryForker.java:196` - **duplication** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** ZoneResource.java:39-51 is the declared single home for where each shareable id sits in a zone header (AREA 0x02, MAP 0x04, TEXT 0x06, SCRIPT 0x18) and offers idIn/setIn/idInMasterRow; its own javadoc says it exists because these "were never written down in one place" and that this cost zones 536-539 sharing Sootopolis's text and area. Measured against that: GeometryForker writes the MAP id with a bare literal at 10 sites - `putU16(newZo, hdrOff + 4, newMatrixIndex);` (196), plus 116, 227, 333, 549, 582, 645, 773, 780, 799 - and uses ZoneResource only once, at 847. AreaForker re-declares the offset at line 63 (`public static final int HDR_AREA_OFF = 2;`) and hand-rolls it at 10 sites (107, 110, 123, 155, 185, 199, 301, 331, 385, 449). WorkspaceIntegrity re-declares BOTH at lines 45-46 (`HDR_AREA_OFF = 2`, `HDR_MATRIX_OFF = 4`). The row stride is declared independently three times - GeometryForker.java:59, WorkspaceIntegrity.java:44, ZoneLimitPatch.java:49, all `0x38` - with two aliases (AreaForker.java:61, ZoneRemover.java:31). Only TextForker.java:97/106/107 goes through ZoneResource. The battery carries four more private copies (AreaShareGuardTest.java:36-37, HandedGameTest.java:154-156, MatrixForkTest.java:47, PackReportTest.java:37), so it holds no independent statement of the layout either.

**What the user gets.** These numbers decide the byte offset at which a u16 is overwritten inside the user's zone headers and the master zone-header table the game boots from. A correction to ZoneResource - or a second game whose header is laid out differently, which CTRMap is explicitly built for - lands on TextForker and nowhere else: AreaForker and GeometryForker keep repointing at the ORAS offsets and silently overwrite a different field of the user's zone header. WorkspaceIntegrity, the detector that tells the user their workspace is consistent, carries its own copy of both numbers, so after such a drift it would clear a workspace the forkers had wrecked.

**Fix.** Make ZoneResource the only statement of the layout: move the row stride onto it, make `headerOffset` private, and leave idIn/setIn/idInMasterRow as the only way to reach it. Delete AreaForker.HDR_AREA_OFF, WorkspaceIntegrity.HDR_AREA_OFF/HDR_MATRIX_OFF and the two spare MASTER_ROW declarations. javac then refuses every hand-rolled site rather than a suite reporting them later.

Its javadoc at 12-25 states the reason verbatim: the four fields "were never written down in one place", and the measured cost was zones 536-539 sharing story text 491, script 134 and area 24 with Sootopolis. (The census called all ten "writes"; four are writes, six are reads — the only inaccuracy I found, and it does not change the claim.) GeometryForker reaches ZoneResource only at 847/867 (`repointMasterRow`), whose own javadoc at 852-856 says the offset "comes from ZoneResource … rather than one per resource" — a rule the same file breaks nine lines-of-arithmetic elsewhere. - `src/ctrmap/AreaForker.java:63` `public static final int HDR_AREA_OFF = 2;` re-declares AREA's offset, used at 107, 110, 123, 155, 185, 199, 301, 331, 385, 449. - `src/ctrmap/Works

## M.47 EncounterTable hand-rolls the container slicer ContainerBytes was written to replace, without its bounds checks

`src/ctrmap/formats/encounters/EncounterTable.java:153` - **duplication** - effort: small

**What the code does.** read() asks ContainerBytes for the count and then slices by hand: `int o0 = i32(enPack, 4 + zone * 4), o1 = i32(enPack, 4 + (zone + 1) * 4); ... byte[] blob = new byte[o1 - o0]; System.arraycopy(enPack, o0, blob, 0, blob.length);` (l.153-158), and write() repeats the same three lines for every other zone (l.178-181). ContainerBytes.subfile does exactly this with the checks — `if (tableEnd > container.length) return null; if (from < 0 || to < from || to > container.length) return null;` (ContainerBytes.java:60-70) — and its class comment says thirty-four hand-written copies of this slicer were folded into it because "they had drifted on what a corrupt table or an empty subfile should answer". write() also stamps `out[0] = 'E'; out[1] = 'N';` (l.190-191) rather than carrying the container's own magic.

**What the user gets.** A truncated or corrupt EN pack gives NegativeArraySizeException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException out of the middle of a rebuild instead of a refusal naming the pack — and this is the pack holding EVERY zone's wild table, written straight back to the workspace and packed (EncounterEditDialog.java:85-92). The thirty-fifth copy of a slicer is also the one a fix to ContainerBytes will not reach.

**Fix.** Slice through ContainerBytes.subfile and refuse in words when it answers null. The capability is already centralised; this caller uses half of it (count) and reimplements the other half.

**Verified.** The source says what is claimed. EncounterTable.java:8 already imports ContainerBytes and calls ContainerBytes.count at :149 and :168, then hand-rolls the slice it stopped short of: `int o0 = i32(enPack, 4 + zone * 4), o1 = i32(enPack, 4 + (zone + 1) * 4); ... byte[] blob = new byte[o1 - o0]; System.arraycopy(enPack, o0, blob, 0, blob.length);` (EncounterTable.java:153-158), repeated verbatim per zone in write() at :178-181. ContainerBytes.subfile is the same slice with the checks — `int tableEnd = HEADER + (i + 2) * 4; if (tableEnd > container.length) return null; ... if (from < 0 || to < from || to > container.length) return null; return Arrays.copyOfRange(container, from, to);` (ContainerBytes.java:62-71) — and its javadoc (ContainerBytes.java:14-18) declares itself the single owner of this job after thirty-four copies were folded in, which is the owner's "duplication of a CAPABILITY" exactly. The unchecked path is reachable and unguarded. LittleEndian.i32 indexes raw (`b[o] & 0xFF | ...`, LittleEndian.java:34-35), and ContainerBytes.count trusts the u16 at offset 2 without bounds (ContainerBytes.java:46-51), so a pack whose declared count outruns its offset table throws AIOOBE at :153 and offsets that run backwards throw NegativeArraySizeException at :157. The project's real refusal for this pack, ZoneAppender.validateEN (ZoneAppender.java:379-408: magic, count, table lengt

## M.48 The patricia name-tree writer exists twice, in two classes that both write bytes into game files

`src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/BchTexturePack.java:694` - **duplication** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** BchTexturePack.java:694-760 (`class Node`, `getBit`, `traverse`, `buildTree`) and BchModelAppender.java:32-63 (`class Node`, `getBit`, `traverse`, `buildTree`) are the same algorithm token for token after normalising whitespace and access modifiers — same root refBit 0xFFFFFFFFL, same `while (root.refBit > left.refBit && left.refBit > bit)` walk, same `while (getBit(...) == getBit(...)) if (--bit < 0) throw`. Only the element type differs (List<Texture> vs List<String>). The other keeps emitting the old tree into either area texture packs (a/0/1/4) or model contents dictionaries (a/0/3/9), which is wrong bytes in the user's ROM files with no local symptom until the game reads the file. This is the exact shape of the message/sign injector duplication already closed by PawnClosure.

**Fix.** One PatriciaTree class taking a List<String> of names, used by both emitters — the same consolidation PawnClosure made for the two Pawn transplants.

I normalised both slices (strip comments/whitespace, unify the element accessor `t.name`->`t`) and they come out 1173 vs 1172 characters with the only residue being `int left; int right;` vs `int left, right;`, single-statement brace style, and the throw message ("duplicate texture name " + t.name vs "dup "+t). Same `root.refBit = 0xFFFFFFFFL`, same `while (root.refBit > left.refBit && left.refBit > bit)` walk, same `long bit = (maxLen << 3) - 1` seed, same `while (getBit(nodes.get(index).name, bit) == getBit(t.name, bit)) if (--bit < 0) throw`, same left/right linking and same rootOut fixup. It is one function written twice. (2) Both paths are live and both write bytes into game files. BchTexturePack: `buildTree` is called from `emit` at BchTexturePack.java:242 ("//patricia name tree" / `List<Node> nodes = buildTree(texes);`), and `emit` is reached by `importIntoArea`/`carryToArea`, called from `PropEditForm.java:638` and `:1094`, `TilePainterForm.java:947`, `GeoEditForm.java:614` — i.e. fro
