## M.107 LZ11.decompress invents data past the end of its input and can never fail

`src/ctrmap/formats/garc/LZ11.java:18` - **silent-failure** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `byte type = (byte) instream.read(); if (type != 0x11) { System.err.println("Not a LZ11 compressed file"); }` - then decompresses anyway. The loop runs `while (currentOutSize < decompressedSize)` on a ByteArrayInputStream that answers -1 forever at EOF. prefixDecodes' own javadoc twelve lines below says it: "at the end of its input it reads -1, takes that as a flag byte with every bit set and a 16-byte run at displacement 0, and pads its way to the declared length - so it 'decodes' anything".

**What the user gets.** A truncated or mis-sniffed entry comes back as a full-size buffer of invented bytes that no caller can distinguish from real data - and the editor re-saves what it parsed from it. The project already measured this poisoning one of its own measurements (the "worst legitimate ratio" figure). ZoneHeader.fetchArchives:263 feeds it area subfile 11 on the one-byte `wTex[0] == 0x11` test, which the same file proves wrong on 13 retail records.

**Fix.** Throw when read() returns -1 mid-stream and when the declared size is not reached; the one caller that must tolerate it (the sniff) already has prefixDecodes. Then route ZoneHeader:261 and GroundMaterialAudit:222 through GARC's measured sniff instead of the byte test.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed, and I read every line. src/ctrmap/formats/garc/LZ11.java:18-21 is verbatim `byte type = (byte) instream.read(); if (type != 0x11) { System.err.println("Not a LZ11 compressed file"); }` with no return and no throw - execution falls straight into the decode loop. That loop, LZ11.java:32-85, is `while (currentOutSize < decompressedSize)` over a ByteArrayInputStream, which answers -1 forever past EOF; -1 becomes a flag byte with every bit set, and the default token arm (LZ11.java:60-65) turns byte1=-1,byte2=-1 into length 16, disp 0, so it copies 16 bytes per iteration out of the 4096-byte window until it reaches the declared length. decompress has no failure exit at all: every path returns outstream.toByteArray(), never null, never an exception. The file's own javadoc states it - LZ11.java:116-119: at EOF it "pads its way to the declared length - so it 'decodes' anything, and a measurement built on it counted 12 raw retail records as compressed." Reachability: the GARC entry channel is CLOSED and I confirmed it - GARC.java:203-204 gates `entries[i].compressed` on `buffer.length >= LZ11.minimumStoredLength(declared) && LZ11.prefixDecodes(buffer, 64)`, and GarcSniffTest (src/ctrmap/tests/GarcSniffTest.java:190-250, 259+) refuses both directions on the retail dump. But two callers bypass that refusal and feed decompress on a one-byte test: src/c

## M.108 The collision reader prints "Coll magic mismatch" and parses the bytes anyway

`src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionFile.java:49` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `if (dis.read4Bytes() != COLL_MAGIC) { System.err.println("Coll magic mismatch"); }` - no return, no throw. Same at line 53 for a declared length past EOF ("will be adjusted on write") and line 73 for a missing "term". It then reads 16 unbounded `lengths[i]` out of those bytes and does `meshes[i] = new GRCollisionMesh(dis, lengths[i])`. GfColl beside it does refuse: `if (!isColl(data)) throw new IllegalArgumentException("not a coll subfile")`.

**What the user gets.** TileMapPanel:567 builds one of these per populated cell and CollEditPanel:210 opens it for editing, so a region whose subfile 2 is not a coll (a multi-layer GR, a forked region, a torn pack) becomes a mesh made of noise that is drawn, walked for height lookups, and saved through GfColl.build over the region's real collision. The console line lands where the shipped build has no console.

**Fix.** Call GfColl.isColl on the subfile and throw with the region named, the way AbstractGamefreakContainer.open now refuses a container that fails verify(). One refusal closes the magic, length and term cases together.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed and nothing upstream or downstream refuses it. src/ctrmap/formats/gfcollision/GRCollisionFile.java:49-51 is `if (dis.read4Bytes() != COLL_MAGIC) { System.err.println("Coll magic mismatch"); }` with no return and no throw - line 52 immediately does `length = dis.readInt()` on the same stream. Line 53-55 is the same shape for a declared length past EOF, and its message ("Coll length exceeds EOF, will be adjusted on write.") states the accept-and-continue outright. Line 67 reads 16 unbounded `lengths[i]` out of those bytes and line 70 does `meshes[i] = new GRCollisionMesh(dis, lengths[i])`; GRCollisionMesh.java:16-33 loops `numVertexes / 3` reading floats and swallows the EOF itself (`catch (IOException e) { e.printStackTrace(); }`), so a short or foreign subfile yields a partial/noise triangle set rather than a refusal. Line 73-75 warns on a missing "term" and carries on. The contrast the census cites is real: GfColl.java:57-60 is `if (!isColl(data)) { throw new IllegalArgumentException("not a coll subfile"); }`. Reachable with no caller guard: TileMapPanel.java:567 `colls[j][i] = new GRCollisionFile(mm.regions.get(j, i));` runs for every populated cell (also TileMapPanel.java:272 single-region, CollEditPanel.java:210 `new GRCollisionFile(f)`), and none of the three calls GfColl.isColl first - while every OTHER reader of subfile 2 in the tree

## M.109 One bad row silently truncates the building palette for the rest of the session

`src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/BuildingCatalog.java:106` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `if (f.length < 14) { continue; }` drops a short row with no word to anyone. Worse, the try at line 95 wraps the WHOLE while loop, so the first `Integer.parseInt` that fails on any of fields 2-13 jumps to `catch (Exception ex) { System.err.println("BuildingCatalog: load " + resource + " failed: " + ex); }` at 134-136 and abandons every remaining row of the file. `entries` was assigned at line 88 before `load` ran, so `entries()` (84-92) caches and returns whatever partial list survived, for the life of the process.

**What the user gets.** A single malformed line in oras_buildings_auto.tsv — a harvester writing a field wrong, an editing accident — makes the rest of the catalogue vanish from the palette. The user sees a shorter list of buildings, cannot tell it is short, and restarting does not help because nothing in the UI reports it.

**Fix.** Parse each row in its own try so one bad row costs one row, count what was rejected, and surface the count where the palette is shown. Do not cache a partial load as if it were complete.

No suite refuses it: BuildingCatalogTest.java:55 only fails on `entries.size() < 20` and then prints `"catalog: " + entries.size() + " entries, " + fails + " failure(s)"` - a catalogue truncated from 3,583 to 500 prints ALL PASS; BatteryHygieneTest.tsvRows (:269-280) counts rows in the FILE TEXT (`line.split("\t").length >= 14`) and never loads BuildingCatalog, so its doc-count check also stays green. Two qualifications the report overstates: the TSVs are classpath resources copied into build\classes by build.ps1:75, and BuildingHarvester (src/ctrmap/tools/BuildingHarvester.java:65) is a main-only dev tool with no UI caller, so only a developer re-harvest or hand edit can produce a malformed row - 

## M.110 A texture read takes whatever bytes happened to be available and reports a picture

`src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/texturing/H3DTexture.java:208` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `byte[] buffer = new byte[textureSize.width * textureSize.height * 4]; in.read(buffer);` — the return value, which says how many bytes were actually read, is dropped, and the partly-filled buffer goes straight into `TextureCodec.decode` on the next line. LittleEndianDataInputStream.readFully exists for precisely this (its javadoc at line 47-56: "THE ONE THAT SHOULD BE USED for reading a record of known length... the tree is full of callers that dropped that answer") and is not used here. The wrapper makes it worse: RandomAccessBAIS.read(byte[]) (lines 65-69) does `position += read` with no check, so at end of stream it SUBTRACTS one from the tracked position, and skip/seek (77-86) add the requested count without looking at what InputStream.skip actually managed.

**What the user gets.** A texture near the end of the pack decodes with a zero-filled tail and is drawn as if it were correct — the same "278 of 431 entries came back pure zero, none null, no exception" failure that item A closed for GARC, reappearing in the texture path. The corrupted `position` then makes every subsequent seek-relative read in that BCH land in the wrong place.

**Fix.** Use readFully here and skipFully in RandomAccessBAIS.skip/seek, so a short read is an EOFException naming the texture rather than a silent half-picture.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED at a different line: the code is src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/texturing/H3DTexture.java:34-41, not :208 (the file is 43 lines long). Lines 34-36 read exactly as claimed: `byte[] buffer = new byte[textureSize.width * textureSize.height * 4];` / `in.read(buffer);` / `textureData = TextureCodec.decode(buffer, ...)` — the count is dropped and the buffer goes straight into decode. The primitive that exists for this is unused: src/ctrmap/LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:48-61 documents readFully as "THE ONE THAT SHOULD BE USED for reading a record of known length... the tree is full of callers that dropped that answer", citing the same 278-of-431 pure-zero GARC measurement. Reachable on every load: src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/BCHFile.java:227-229 constructs `new H3DTexture(in, buf)` once per texture-pointer-table entry for every BCH the editor opens. NOT guarded: no suite under src/ctrmap/tests feeds a truncated BCH — TexturePackImportTest.java:383-406 only asserts decode byte-identity across import on well-formed packs, and OUTSTANDING.md:113-115 scopes the closed item A to "the container and both GARC entry readers", never the H3D path, so this is a new instance rather than the ratchet. THREE PARTS OF THE CLAIM ARE REFUTED, which is why I rate it medium not high: 1. The cascade ("every subsequent seek-relative read in that BCH lands in the wrong place") is false. RandomAccessBAIS.se

## M.113 The prop registry is marked clean before it is written, so a failed write is lost twice

`src/ctrmap/formats/propdata/ADPropRegistry.java:111` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `public void write(){ if (modified == false) return; modified = false;` (109-111) - the flag is cleared before the try block that assembles and calls `f.storeFile(0, ...)` (120). The IOException catch logs SEVERE and returns (121-123); storeFile's IllegalStateException is not caught here at all. The caller is PropEditForm.store, `reg.write();` followed by `return true;` (humaninterface/PropEditForm.java:851-853). NPCRegistry.store, 30 lines away in the same layer, gets this right and says why: "reported after the write, not before: a mark taken first and then a failed write would send the next pack an unwritten file".

**What the user gets.** An area whose AreaData file is held open (emulator, virus scanner - the failure this project documents as real) loses every prop-registry edit: write() swallows it, store() answers true, the registry believes it is clean so the next Save writes nothing, and the pack ships the old registry. The user is told the prop registry was saved.

**Fix.** Clear `modified` only after storeFile returns, and let the failure out: make write() throw (it has no other way to answer) so PropEditForm.store can report it the way it already reports the no-map-open case at 828.

**Verified.** ADPropRegistry.java:110-111 is verbatim `if (modified == false) return;` then `modified = false;`, ten lines before the write at :120 `f.storeFile(0, baos.toByteArray());`. storeFile writes the container to disk and THROWS on failure (AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:346 `throw new IllegalStateException("could not write subfile " + num + ...)`), against the precondition its own javadoc names at :272 ("an emulator or a virus scanner holding the archive open"). Reachable: ADPropRegistryEditor.java:172/464/478 and PropEditForm.java:491/1128 set reg.modified=true; PropEditForm.java:843-851 is the only write (`if (reg != null && reg.modified) { ... reg.write(); }`); Ui.java:362 `if (!dialog) return Keep.SAVE;` means File > Save (CtrmapMainframe.java:1444 `openEditors.saveAll(false)`) reaches it with no prompt. No caller handles the throw: OpenEditors.java:67-74 saveAll has no try, saveAllAction has none, and there is no setDefaultUncaughtExceptionHandler anywhere in src/. So after a refused write the registry is marked clean, and the next File > Save returns at ADPropRegistry.java:110 having written nothing and said nothing. The correct ordering is spelled out 30 lines away in the same layer: NPCRegistry.java:215-218 "reported after the write, not before: a mark taken first and then a failed write would send the next pack an unwritten file", with files.edited(f); modified = false; AF

## M.115 An opcode the disassembler does not know is announced on stdout and then disassembled anyway

`src/ctrmap/formats/scripts/PawnInstruction.java:580` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `getDisassembly`'s switch opens with `default: System.out.println("Invalid Command ID " + (c & 0x7FFF));` and deliberately falls through into `op = eA(ins);`. `eA` does `Commands.values()[customCommand]` (line 901) and the enum holds 216 constants, so the method's own javadoc claim at lines 571-573 - "an unknown opcode is reported and then printed as a plain mnemonic" - holds only for 0..215; at 216 or above the PawnInstruction constructor dies with an unnamed ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException instead.

**What the user gets.** A script CTRMap cannot decode is handed to the user as decoded text, and the only report goes to a console no user has - the same shape OUTSTANDING item A removed from GARC.open ("rather than printing to a console no user has"). Combined with the short read above, a truncated script decodes as thousands of NONE cells and prints one line each, while the editor shows a normal-looking disassembly and offers Commit.

**Fix.** Record the invalid opcode and its address on the script and refuse: a disassembly containing an opcode this table does not name must not reach the editor as text, because the assembler cannot turn it back into the same bytes.

(1) The stdout-only report. src/ctrmap/formats/scripts/PawnInstruction.java:578-591: `switch (c & 0x7FFF) { default: System.out.println("Invalid Command ID " + (c & 0x7FFF)); case 0x01: ... { op = eA(ins); break; }` - no `break` after the println, so an opcode the table does not know is announced to stdout and then rendered as a normal instruction line. The javadoc at :571-573 states the fall-through is deliberate. That the console is invisible is proven by the tree's own guard: SourceSeamTest:26 and :658 forbid `System.setOut`/`setErr` anywhere in source ("redirects System.out/err"), so nothing routes it to the editor's output box - the same shape OUTSTANDING.md:119 removed from GARC.open ("refuses a file that fails it rather than printing to a console no user has"). PawnInstruction.java:580 and PawnDisassembler.java:77 are the only two System.out.println calls left in ctrmap.formats.scripts. (2) The unnamed crash above the enum. getCommand() is `cellValue & 0x7FFF` (PawnInstruction.java:168-170), so the switch value ranges 0..32767. eA resolves the mnemonic with `String cmd = Commands.values()[customCommand].toString();` (PawnInstruction.java:901). I counted the enum: 216 constants between :980 and :1198, last is NUM_OPCODES (:1197), i.e. legal indices 0..215. Any cell with low-15

The null it converts is reachable: TextFile.java:122 is `if (num >= lines.length) return null;`, and parentMap is a 10-bit field (ZoneHeader.java:108 `parentMap = H0x1C & 0x3FF;` -> 0..1023) while the name table is bounded by the game's ~356 place-id limit (ZoneManager.java:144). A user can put it out of range by hand: the Parent map field is a bare NumberFormatter with no min/max (ZoneLoadingPanel.java:875) and is written straight into the header at ZoneLoadingPanel.java:375 `zone.header.parentMap = (Integer) parentMap.getValue();` with no bound check. The codebase already knows the case is real - ZoneManager.java:136-138 throws `"Zone " + zoneIndex + "'s name line " + curParent + " is out of range."`. No caller repairs the string: ZoneLoadingPanel.java:249/1630/1743, WarpEditForm.java:36/72, PropEditForm.java:690, MatrixEditForm.java:84 concatenate the return value straight into a label. Worse than cosmetic in one place: ZoneRepurposeScanner.java:105-111 `safeName` tests `n == null` - dead code, since getLocName never returns null - so a nameless zone comes back as the literal "NullPointerException"

## M.118 sampleBaseY swallows a corrupt collision and answers "no ground anywhere"

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/PaintedHeights.java:237` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `GfColl c; try { c = new GfColl(donorColl); } catch (RuntimeException ex) { return out; }` where `out` is the all-NaN grid filled at :230-231. GfColl's constructor throws AIOOBE on a file with the right magic and a short body (GfColl.java:66 `System.arraycopy(data, 0x20, boundsRaw, 0, 640)`). Not an empty or log-only catch, so it is outside SilentCatchTest's ceiling. The SAME input hits PaintedCollision.java:151 `template = new GfColl(donorColl);` with no try at all.

**What the user gets.** Every tile's elevation frame quietly becomes the flat baseline (floorYOf returns base0 + h*STEP for NaN), so a composite paint lays its floors, its walls and its collision at the wrong height across the whole region while Apply reports success - and the identical file throws an uncaught AIOOBE out of the collision builder, so one corrupt region both crashes and degrades depending on which stage runs first.

**Fix.** One decode of the donor collision per build, at the top, that throws with the region named; sampleBaseY takes the decoded GfColl rather than raw bytes, so there is no second place that can decide a broken file means "flat ground".

**Verified.** The line is there as quoted. PaintedHeights.java:236-241 reads `GfColl c; try { c = new GfColl(donorColl); } catch (RuntimeException ex) { return out; }`, and `out` is the DIM x DIM grid filled with Float.NaN at :229-232 - byte-identical to the "there is no collision here" answer returned at :233-235. The catch is reachable: GfColl.isColl (GfColl.java:53-55) validates only `b.length >= 0x20` plus the four magic bytes, while the constructor at GfColl.java:66 does `System.arraycopy(data, 0x20, boundsRaw, 0, 640)` (needs 0x2A0 bytes) and then reads the bucket table and vertices through i32/f32, which LittleEndian.java:16-18 documents as throwing AIOOBE past the end ("they throw ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException for an offset past the end"). So any coll subfile with the right magic and a short or garbled body parses as valid to isColl and throws inside the constructor. Downstream, floorYOf (PaintedHeights.java:219-222) turns every NaN into `base0 + h*STEP`, and base0 is 0f because baseFloor returns `Float.isNaN(min) ? 0f : min` (:208), so the whole region's frame collapses to absolute h*STEP rather than the region's real surface. The one channel that could tell the user reports nothing: nearestGround's own javadoc says "A region with no sample at all stays NaN throughout" (:151), so borrowedGroundTiles (:170-181, requires `!Float.isNaN(ground)`) returns 0 and the Apply dialog's borrow

## M.119 A half-parsed zone header is written back over the real one on the next save

`src/ctrmap/formats/zone/ZoneHeader.java:153` - **silent-failure** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** The constructor reads 0x2C bytes of fields and ends `} catch (IOException ex) { Logger.getLogger(ZoneHeader.class.getName()).log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex); }` - every field after the truncation point keeps its Java default. `mapType = dis.read(); mapMove = dis.read();` (lines 93-94) do not even throw: read() answers -1 at EOF, which assembleData writes back as 0xFF. Zone.changed() (Zone.java:50) then compares `header.assembleData()` with `file.getFile(0)`, sees they differ, and Zone.store (Zone.java:100) calls `file.storeFile(0, header.assembleData())`.

**What the user gets.** Open a zone whose header subfile is short or torn, touch nothing, save: the editor writes a header of zeros and 0xFFs over the game's real one and reports a save. This is the defect item J closed for ctrmap.formats.cameradata, in the reader one layer up.

**Fix.** Let the constructor throw, as CameraDataFile does - the zone panel already reports a throw from the load path - and use readFully/readUnsignedByte so an exhausted stream is an EOFException rather than -1.

**Verified.** The mechanism is in the source exactly as claimed, though the claimed TRIGGER is half wrong. Confirmed: src/ctrmap/formats/zone/ZoneHeader.java:153-155 ends the 0x38-byte parse with `} catch (IOException ex) { Logger.getLogger(ZoneHeader.class.getName()).log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex); }` — every field after the cut keeps its Java default and the object is handed back as if it parsed. (3) That `toolPane` is bound at `src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:610` `CtrmapMainframe::switchToolUI`, and `switchToolUI` (`src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:1284-1299`) replaces the pane with no question: `jsp.setRightComponent(scroll); adjustSplitPanes(); frame.revalidate();`. The same host is reached again for every other tool via the ToolHost at `src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:670-673` `public void showToolUi(JComponent form) { switchToolUI(form); }`, so leaving the pane is one tool-button click. ## M.121 "Cannot tell whether this area is shared" is treated as "it is not shared"

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/AreaForkPrompt.java:69` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `try { sharers = AreaForker.areaSharers(zoneIndex); } catch (Exception ex) { return unforked(zoneIndex, currentArea); //cannot tell - let the edit proceed as before }` - unforked() answers `forked = false` and hands back the zone's CURRENT (possibly shared) area id as the one to write into.

**What the user gets.** This class is, in its own words, "the last thing standing between a paint and 400-odd maps it was never meant to touch". When the sharer count cannot be read, the user is never shown the question, the edit goes straight into the shared area, and every other zone sharing it silently changes - fog, water animations, prop registry, NPC models - with no record that the check failed. 77% of retail zones share an area, so the failure mode is the common case.

**Fix.** Refuse instead of assuming: report that the sharing could not be determined and return null (cancel), the same answer the class already gives when the user cancels. "Unknown" must not resolve to the permissive branch on a decision whose whole point is protecting data the user did not open.

**Verified.** Code is verbatim as claimed. src/ctrmap/humaninterface/AreaForkPrompt.java:67-71 — `try { sharers = AreaForker.areaSharers(zoneIndex); } catch (Exception ex) { return unforked(zoneIndex, currentArea); //cannot tell - let the edit proceed as before }` — and unforked() (same file, :107-114) sets `r.newArea = area; r.forked = false;`, i.e. the caller is handed the zone's CURRENT (possibly shared) area as the id to write into, with no question asked and nothing reported. The failure path is genuinely reachable: GARC.parse swallows into an empty entry table (src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:56), so getDecompressedEntry returns null (GARC.java:677-680) and `fo.write(entry)` at BuildingPaletteDialog.java:130 throws NPE into that catch. No caller recovers: BuildingPaletteDialog.java:298 is a bare `texes.addAll(donorTextures(e.donorArea))`, and PaintForm.java:871's `catch (Exception ignore)` never fires because donorTextures swallows rather than throws. The only trace is System.err, and System.setErr is forbidden repo-wide (SourceSeamTest.java:26), so a GUI user sees nothing. CORRECTION TO THE CLAIMED HARM: this is preview-only. CameraData.equals (CameraData.java:123) compares movementDirection, so `!cam.equals(cam2)` at line 239 is true and lines 241-244 swap the record in, rewrite unknown01or03 to 0x03, and set f.modified = true. NO EDIT IS NEEDED TO TRIGGER IT: entryBoxActionPerformed (CameraEditForm.java:897-899) calls saveCamera() merely on selecting a different camera in the list, and showCamera(entryNum, true) calls saveCamera(false) at line 111 — so browsing the camera dropdown rewrites the previously shown camera. ## M.124 The headless progress wait drops its timeout answer, so a slow job continues as though it finished

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/LoadingDialog.java:79` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `closed.await(2, TimeUnit.MINUTES);` (79) - CountDownLatch.await returns false when it times out, and the value is discarded; showDialog then returns normally (83) and the caller carries on. The comment beside it only argues for the timeout's existence: 'a test run that stops is a result, one that hangs is not.'

**What the user gets.** In a headless run - which is how the guard battery drives packWorkspace, zone loads and archive rewrites - a job that takes over two minutes lets showDialog return while the worker is still writing, and everything after it (Workspace.packWorkspace:570 goes straight on to reportPackWarnings) runs against a half-written workspace and reports success. A suite that times out cannot tell itself apart from a suite that passed.

**Fix.** Use the answer: throw or fail loudly with the description that was on the bar when the wait expires, so a timeout is distinguishable from completion.

**Verified.** LoadingDialog.java:79 reads `closed.await(2, TimeUnit.MINUTES);` — the boolean return (false on timeout) is discarded, the only catch (80) is InterruptedException, and the method falls through to `return;` at 83, so a headless showDialog that timed out is indistinguishable from one whose worker closed it. The path is reachable: makeDialog:57 leaves dlg null only when GraphicsEnvironment.isHeadless(), and PackReportTest.java:30 sets java.awt.headless=true as its first statement precisely so the battery drives this branch. The caller does not compensate — Workspace.java:570-573 is `progress.showDialog();` immediately followed by `reportPackWarnings(Ui.parent(), warnings);` under a comment asserting "showDialog returns on the EDT once done() has closed the progress dialog, so this is the first moment anything else can be seen", and `warnings` (Workspace.java:539) is a plain ArrayList still being filled by doInBackground:563, so a timeout reads it mid-write and reports a clean pack. ZoneLoadingPanel.java:323 and :1298 and TileMapPanel.java:1014 also return with nothing after the wait. Only TileMapPanel.java:441-445 handles it, following showDialog with an unconditional worker.get(). With a display, dlg.setVisible(true) (88) blocks on the modal pump with no timeout, so the divergence is headless-only.

**What the user gets.** The user types '3, 5x, 7', presses Enter, and the cell snaps back to what it was. Nothing says which token was rejected or that anything was rejected at all, so the natural reading is that the table saved it - and this row decides which teams a trainer class fields in every facility.

**Fix.** Say which token was refused, at the point the edit is refused. Rejecting silently is the same failure mode as saving silently.

**Verified.** Code is verbatim as claimed. src/ctrmap/humaninterface/MaisonClassListDialog.java:284-288 — inside ListModel.setValueAt's token loop: `try { parsed.add(Math.max(0, Math.min(0xFFFF, Integer.parseInt(tok)))); } catch (NumberFormatException ignore) { return; // reject the whole edit on any bad token }`. The return happens BEFORE the commit at :290-293 (`l.setIndices.clear(); l.setIndices.addAll(parsed); dirty = true; fireTableRowsUpdated(r, r);`), so the model keeps the old list and the cell repaints from getValueAt (:241-255) with the old comma string. No ctrmap.Ui call, no flag, no exception — the method is void and the JTable caller cannot see the refusal. REACHABLE: the table is a plain `new JTable(model)` at :77 with no custom editor, no getColumnClass and no InputVerifier (the model overrides only getColumnName/isCellEditable/getValueAt/setValueAt, :230-294), so the default JTextField editor accepts any text and hands "3, 5x, 7" straight to setValueAt; the panel is reached from the shipped "Class assignments..." button in src/ctrmap/humaninterface/MaisonEditDialog.java:171. NO CALLER HANDLES IT: the only other entry points are the Save button (:126-137) which just calls model.save() and then reports `"Saved " + TABLE_NAMES[...] + ".\nDeploy to emulator to apply."` — a success dialog over the row the edit never reached. `src/ctrmap/humaninterface/NPCEditForm.java:708` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** Identical four lines at 707-710 (getStoryFile), 778-781 (loadWrapperDonor) and 809-812 (loadSignDonor): `InputStream in = new FileInputStream(f); byte[] b = new byte[in.available()]; in.read(b); in.close();`. The return of read() is discarded, available() is not a file length, and close() is not in a finally. The tree already has both primitives: `java.nio.file.Files.readAllBytes` is used at CtrmapMainframe.java:2659 and 3193, and OUTSTANDING item A added LittleEndianDataInputStream.readFully for exactly this class of hand-rolled short read.

Downstream it is caught - GFMessageFile.parse refuses a truncated buffer at `sectionDataOffset + totalLength != data.length`, and extractZoneScript bounds-checks - so the story-text path degrades to "Story text file N could not be read" and the donor scan silently skips that zone, which is why this is not a corruption today; it is the same defect the project swept out of the container readers, still present in the UI, with an IOException from read() additionally leaking the handle (the donor scan runs this over all 536 zones).

**Fix.** One call - Files.readAllBytes(f.toPath()) - at all three sites; it cannot short-read and cannot leak. Deleting the idiom is what stops a fourth copy being written.

All three are reachable from the editor: getStoryFile at 558, 678, 839, 990, 1075, 1251; loadWrapperDonor at 970, 1262; loadSignDonor at 1057. No suite refuses it — grep over src/ctrmap/tests finds no scan for this pattern, and SilentCatchTest counts only EMPTY and LOG_ONLY catches (SilentCatchTest.java:50 `CEILING = 119`, :86 LOG_ONLY matcher) while these catches carry statements, so they are not inside the ratchet. ## M.129 Failure reports splice a getMessage() that is null for a whole family of exceptions, instead of the tree's Ui.reason

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/PaintForm.java:1071` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** applyFailed - the documented "only account of what an Apply did" - reports `"Apply failed:\n" + why.getMessage() + "\n\nNothing was written..."` (1071-1072), and it is the catch-all for `catch (Exception ex) { applyFailed(ex); }` around the whole apply chain (1047-1049). Ui.reason exists for exactly this and says so: "a site that forgot it showed null" (Ui.java:199-212). Same pattern at PaintForm:516 (`"Could not add the animation:\n" + ex.getMessage()`), PropEditForm:1138, TilePainterForm:1188, and into the Apply report at TilePainterForm:886 and :1078. DialogSeamTest's rule four does not catch them: it tests `BARE_MESSAGE.matcher(body).matches()` (DialogSeamTest:382), which only fires when the ENTIRE message argument is a getMessage() call, so a concatenated one passes.

**What the user gets.** Any NullPointerException escaping applyToZone - and it is a long chain over parsed game data - reaches the user as "Apply failed: null. Nothing was written." The one sentence the user gets about a refused write to their map says nothing, and there is no console in the shipped app to fall back on.

**Fix.** `ctrmap.Ui.reason(why)` at all six sites. To close the class rather than the instances, widen DialogSeamTest's BARE_MESSAGE check from matches() to find() over the message argument, so a getMessage() anywhere inside a Ui report is refused - the suite already reads that argument.

**Verified.** Confirmed line by line; I tried each refutation route and none holds. 1. The code says it. src\ctrmap\humaninterface\PaintForm.java:1071-1072 — `ctrmap.Ui.error(this, "Apply failed:\n" + why.getMessage() + "\n\nNothing was written - the map is exactly as it was.", "Map Builder");` — and it is the sole catch-all for the whole apply chain at PaintForm.java:1047-1049 (`catch (Exception ex) { applyFailed(ex); }` around `TilePainterForm.applyToZone(...)` at :1042). 2. The path is reachable with a null message. Every deliberate `throw` inside applyToZone carries text (TilePainterForm.java:161, 174, 209, 496, 547, 912, 1046...), so the only exceptions that reach applyFailed with a null message are the unplanned ones — exactly the family Ui's own doc names. The tree has already been bitten by it and wrote it down: CtrmapMainframe.java:2212-2216 — "a container that is not a map matrix usually fails on a bare dereference, whose getMessage() is null - 'null' in a dialog is indistinguishable from the editor ignoring the click" — and that site was fixed to `Ui.reason(ex)`. 3. No caller or seam rescues it. Ui.saying (Ui.java:126-127) only substitutes when the WHOLE text is null or blank: `return text == null || text.trim().isEmpty() ? NOTHING_SAID : text;`. "Apply failed:\nnull\n\nNothing was written..." is neither, so it renders verba

**What the user gets.** When importBrushMaterials or buildModelOnly starts failing - a brush whose donor material cannot be imported into this map, say - the user keeps painting against a picture of an earlier state that the Apply will not reproduce. Nothing is said anywhere, not even to stderr, and the first report is an Apply that fails on the same exception.

**Fix.** Keep the last good preview, but say the preview is stale: a line in the form (the zoneLabel or placeStatus already carry status text) naming the failure, cleared by the next successful regen. A preview that silently stops tracking the document is the same class of defect as a dialog that reports a write that did not happen.

## M.139 A dump whose archive cannot be opened is reported as an incomplete unpack

`src/ctrmap/setup/DumpCheck.java:314` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** looksLikeArchive ends `} catch (IOException ex) { return false; }`, and a false from it puts the archive's friendly name into `broken` (line 223), which prints "These are present but unreadable: ... The unpacking probably did not finish - try unpacking your game again." (lines 238-240). The same swallow makes fillIfDump return false (line 264), which downgrades a real dump to WRONG_FOLDER or NOT_A_DUMP.

**What the user gets.** An archive that is present and fine but momentarily unopenable - held by an emulator, on a disconnected network share, denied by permissions - is reported as damage to the dump, and the user is told to re-unpack a 1.8 GB game to fix a lock. The exception that said which it was is discarded.

**Fix.** Keep the reason and distinguish "wrong magic" from "could not open": the first is damage, the second is a sentence naming the file and the error, which is a different instruction to the user.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED as written. src/ctrmap/setup/DumpCheck.java:314-316 is literally `} catch (IOException ex) { return false; }` inside looksLikeArchive, discarding ex entirely. That false reaches two places. (1) DumpCheck.java:222-224, `else if (!looksLikeArchive(f)) { broken.add(friendly(t)); }` — reached only after :220 already proved `f.isFile() && f.length() != 0`, so "the file is absent or empty" is excluded and the only remaining meanings are "wrong magic" or "could not open/read", which the code conflates. The result is DumpCheck.java:231-240: status DAMAGED, headline "That looks like ... but part of it is missing." (wrong on its face when `missing` is empty), detail "These are present but unreadable: X. The unpacking probably did not finish - try unpacking your game again." (2) DumpCheck.java:264, `if (!f.isFile() || f.length() == 0 || !looksLikeArchive(f)) return false;` in fillIfDump, which isDump (:277) and thus findLikelyDumps (:369) and the WRONG_FOLDER suggestion paths (:130, :295) all depend on — a locked dump is silently not found and not offered. Reachability is real given the :220/:264 guards: `new RandomAccessFile(f,"r")` throws FileNotFoundException (an IOException) on a Windows sharing violation or a denied ACL even though isFile()/length() succeed; readFully throws on a share that drops mid-read; and because the body returns, an IOException from the implicit try-w

## M.140 Setup replaces a real load failure with a diagnosis that sends the user to re-unpack their game

`src/ctrmap/setup/SetupWizard.java:592` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** completeSetup does `int zones = 0; try { zones = ctrmap.CtrmapMainframe.onWorkspaceOpened(Workspace.validate(this, false)); } catch (RuntimeException ex) { zones = 0; }` - the exception and its message are dropped on the floor - and then the zones==0 branch reports "CTRMap read that folder but found no maps in it.\n\nThe unpacked game is probably incomplete - unpacking sometimes stops early without saying so. Try unpacking your game again".

**What the user gets.** Any RuntimeException from validate/onWorkspaceOpened - a permission error on the workspace, a corrupt archive, a NPE in a reader - is reported as "your dump is incomplete", sending a first-time user off to re-dump a cartridge and re-unpack 1.8 GB to fix something that was never about their dump. The one piece of evidence that would have told them apart was in the caught exception.

**Fix.** Keep the throwable and say which happened: zero zones from a clean load is "no maps found"; a throw is "CTRMap could not open that game" with Ui.reason(ex) attached. The catch exists only because the return value could not distinguish them - so let the failure carry its own cause instead of being flattened into a count.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what was claimed and I could not refute it. ## M.141 Two suites' javadoc says they skip "rather than passing", and both print ALL PASS and exit 0

`src/ctrmap/tests/PlantLedgerTest.java:58` - **silent-failure** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** PlantLedgerTest.java:41 promises "Needs python, and says so and skips rather than passing if it is missing", and lines 58-64 do `if (!CommitGuardTest.onPath("python")) { println(" skip: ..."); println("ALL PASS"); return; }`. CommitGuardTest.java:33 makes the same promise and lines 48-53 do the same thing. test.ps1 judges a suite solely by $LASTEXITCODE (line 291), so "ALL PASS" plus exit 0 IS passing. On a machine with no python: the plant ledger's shape check, the commit gate's eleven refusal proofs, the work-order gate (test.ps1:33-34, "(no python on PATH - the work order cannot be asked)") and the mutation harness selftest (test.ps1:306) all go silent, and the run still ends ALL SUITES PASS.

**What the user gets.** Four of the guards that guard the guards can be absent from a run with nothing in the verdict saying so, and the suites' own documentation asserts that cannot happen. Anyone reading the javadoc believes the battery distinguishes skipped from passed; it does not.

**Fix.** Give the battery a skip channel: a suite that could not run prints a token, test.ps1 counts them, and a run with any skip is reported as such instead of ALL SUITES PASS. That is the same refusal the pristine-archive check already makes, moved to run time.

**Verified.** Confirmed at every cited line. PlantLedgerTest.java:40-41 javadoc: "Needs python, and says so and skips rather than passing if it is missing" — but PlantLedgerTest.java:58-63 is `if (!CommitGuardTest.onPath("python")) { println(" skip: no python on PATH..."); println("ALL PASS"); return; }` — a `return` from `main`, i.e. exit 0, with theLedgerStillMatchesTheTree() and whatItDoesNotCoverIsCountedAndNamed() (lines 65-66) never called. CommitGuardTest.java:32-33 makes the same promise ("says so and skips if either is missing, rather than passing") and CommitGuardTest.java:48-53 does the identical skip+"ALL PASS"+return, skipping all eleven refusal proofs at lines 55-67. test.ps1:129-130 registers both suites, and test.ps1:291 is the whole verdict: `if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) { $failed += $s.n }` — exit 0 IS passing, so the run ends "ALL SUITES PASS" (test.ps1:322). The other two python-gated checks also fall through on such a machine: test.ps1:26-34 prints "(no python on PATH - the work order cannot be asked)" and continues, and test.ps1:304-310 prints "skip: no python on PATH" for the mutation-harness selftest without touching $failed. No suite refuses this: BatteryHygieneTest is the suite that exists for exactly this class (its javadoc at :28-34 and :147-156 records ZoneLimitPatchTest/BchMapModelTest/MaisonClassListTest "printed SKIP ... Green, silent, and asserting nothing"), yet

## M.142 The harvester counts a thrown exception and a rejected candidate as the same thing

`src/ctrmap/tools/BuildingHarvester.java:207` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** The verification loop ends `} catch (Exception ex) { dropped++; }` and the only report is "verified N entries (M dropped)" (line 215). A candidate rejected by the gate (p == null, a skinned piece, a failed validate) increments the same counter from lines 177/190/194.

**What the user gets.** A regression in MapPrefab.extract or BchMapModel.validate shows up as a smaller catalogue, not as an error, in the one run that decides what ships in oras_buildings_auto.tsv. Nobody can tell a harvest that rejected 200 honest candidates from one where 200 throws went by.

**Fix.** Count and print them apart - dropped by the gate vs threw, with the first few exceptions named - so the run's own numbers say whether the sweep worked.

**Verified.** BuildingHarvester.java:207-209 is literally `} catch (Exception ex) { dropped++; }` with `ex` never read, and the same `dropped` counter is incremented by the three gate rejections at lines 177 (`p == null`), 190 (`!whole`) and 194 (validate non-empty); the only report is line 215 `"verified " + verified + " entries (" + dropped + " dropped)"`. The throw path is reachable, not speculative: MapPrefab.extract throws IllegalStateException at src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/MapPrefab.java:183, stampGeometry throws IllegalStateException("appended mesh not found") at MapPrefab.java:569, and `new BchMapModel(sr.newModel)` at BuildingHarvester.java:193 throws IllegalArgumentException("not a BCH") per src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/BchMapModel.java:105 - all three become an ordinary "dropped" candidate. main() is declared `throws Exception` (line 59), so this catch is exactly what turns an abort into a silent shrinkage of the one run that produces the committed oras_buildings_auto.tsv, with exit code 0.

## M.143 The app-image update applies with no rollback and reports the failure to a log file

`src/ctrmap/update/Updater.java:456` - **silent-failure** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** runApply does `copyOver(payload, target, backup, "", done); ... System.out.println("CTRMap: update applied.");` and on failure `catch (IOException ex) { System.out.println("CTRMap: could not apply the update: " + ex.getMessage()); return; }` (453-458). `done` - the list of files already replaced - is collected (452) and never read. Its sibling applyStaged, 140 lines above, catches the same throw and restores every entry of `done` before rethrowing (321-338). stdout here is redirected to `apply.log` (startAppImageApply, 424).

**What the user gets.** A copy that fails half way through leaves the user's installed program part old and part new, with no restore, and the method returns before the relaunch - so the app simply never comes back up. Everything the user is told lives in a log file inside a hidden folder. Two implementations of "apply the staged update", one of which has the rollback.

**Fix.** One apply: runApply should call the same body applyStaged uses (restore from `done`, rethrow), and write the failure where the launcher can show it rather than to stdout.

Files.copy(from.toPath(), new File(installDir, rel[0]).toPath(), REPLACE_EXISTING); }` then rethrows "The update could not be applied, so the previous version was put back." — two implementations of one job, rollback on only one. On failure runApply:456-458 is `catch (IOException ex) { System.out.println("CTRMap: could not apply the update: " + ex.getMessage()); return; }`; that `return` precedes the relaunch block at 460-467, so the app never restarts. stdout is a log file: startAppImageApply:423-424 `pb.redirectErrorStream(true); pb.redirectOutput(new File(stageRoot(installDir), "apply.log"));` with stageRoot = `new File(installDir, ".ctrmap-update")` (218-219), and runApply's javadoc says "Never shows a window." ## M.144 A vault whose manifest will not parse reports as no vault at all

`src/ctrmap/vault/VaultManifest.java:174` - **silent-failure** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** read() ends `} catch (IOException | RuntimeException ex) { return null; }` - and the body parses with `Long.parseLong(p[0])`, `Long.parseLong(v)` and `Integer.parseInt(v)` on lines it has not validated, so one malformed byte anywhere in the file throws and the whole manifest becomes null. Every caller reads null as absence: Vault.restoreOne "There is no finished backup to restore from." (line 543), restoreAll the same (597), Vault.list() silently drops the entry (644-645), and VaultUi.restoreEverything says "There is no backup of this game to restore from." (line 148).

**What the user gets.** The class javadoc says a partial seal is the dangerous state and must be visible. A CORRUPT manifest is the same state and is invisible: the files are all there, sealed and verified, and the editor reports that nothing was ever backed up - the one message that will make a user stop looking for it. isInterrupted() cannot help, because the marker was deleted when the seal finished.

**Fix.** Separate the three answers the caller needs: no manifest, an unfinished seal, and a manifest that is present but will not parse. Make read() throw (or return a result carrying whyNot, the shape GARC.verify already uses in this tree) so "damaged manifest" reaches the user with the entry folder named, and the files stay restorable by hand.

**Verified.** Every claimed line is there as quoted. VaultManifest.java:174 is `} catch (IOException | RuntimeException ex) { return null; }`, and the body parses unvalidated text at :134 `e.size = Long.parseLong(p[0]);`, :135 `e.mtime = Long.parseLong(p[1]);`, :154 `m.sealedAt = Long.parseLong(v);` and :164 `declared = Integer.parseInt(v);` - a NumberFormatException is a RuntimeException, so one bad digit anywhere collapses the whole manifest to null, the same value :107 returns for "no manifest file at all". Callers confirmed: Vault.java:543 and :597 "There is no finished backup to restore from.", Vault.java:645 `if (m != null)` silently drops the entry from list(), VaultUi.java:148 "There is no backup of this game to restore from.". The state IS reachable and the code itself says so - VaultManifest.java:167-173 already has a hand-written refusal for a truncated manifest ("Refuse it") that returns null down the same path. The strongest evidence is one flow contradicting itself: CtrmapMainframe.java:2402 gates the restore menu on `if (!ctrmap.vault.Vault.isSealed(entry))`, and isSealed is only `VaultManifest.file(entry).isFile()` (Vault.java:156), so with a corrupt manifest the gate says a backup EXISTS, opens the "What should be put back?" dialog, and then CtrmapMainframe.java:2423 -> VaultUi.restoreEverything tells the user at VaultUi.java:148 that there is no backup at all. isInterrupted

## L.1 Workspace.createWorkspace - the one named entry point for making a workspace - is called by nothing and persists nothing

`src/ctrmap/Workspace.java:72` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `public static void createWorkspace(String wspath, String gamepath, String espicapath, boolean tilesetDefault, String customTilesetPath)` (line 72) has zero call sites in src/ (grep "createWorkspace" returns only the declaration). Its whole body assigns the five statics and returns - no prefs write, no validate(), no session install. The real paths bypass it and assign the same statics inline: SetupWizard.java:510 `Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH = wsPath;`, SetupWizard.java:536-537, WorkspaceSettings.java:80 and :139 - and then rely on saveWorkspace() (line 618) which is what actually calls prefs.put/flush and current.savePersist().

**What the user gets.** The obvious API for "set up a workspace" is a trap: a caller who uses it gets an application that looks configured (isConfigured() answers true off those same statics) but has written nothing to preferences and installed no session, so the setting vanishes at restart and Workspace.session() stays null. Meanwhile three call sites hand-roll the assignment, so the job has no owner.

**Fix.** Delete createWorkspace, or make it the only way to set those five statics (fields private, setter validates and calls saveWorkspace) so SetupWizard and WorkspaceSettings cannot assign them directly and a half-made workspace is not expressible.

**Verified.** Verified line by line, and the claim's facts hold exactly. src/ctrmap/Workspace.java:72-78 is the whole method: `public static void createWorkspace(String wspath, String gamepath, String espicapath, boolean tilesetDefault, String customTilesetPath)` whose body is five assignments (`WORKSPACE_PATH = wspath;` ... `TILESET_PATH = customTilesetPath;`) and nothing else - no prefs.put, no validate(), no install(). It carries no javadoc at all (line 71 is blank, line 72 is the signature). `grep -rn "createWorkspace" .` over the whole tree returns exactly two hits: the declaration at src/ctrmap/Workspace.java:72 and the stale ./build/classes/ctrmap/Workspace.class - zero call sites in src/, production or tests, and no reflection or script reference. The live paths do hand-roll the same assignment: src/ctrmap/humaninterface/WorkspaceSettings.java:80-83 (`Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH = wsPathField.getText();` + ESPICA_PATH/TILESET_DEFAULT/TILESET_PATH), WorkspaceSettings.java:139 (`Workspace.GAMEDIR_PATH = gameField.getText();`), src/ctrmap/setup/SetupWizard.java:510 (`Workspace.WORKSPACE_PATH = wsPath;`) and SetupWizard.java:536-537 - then persistence happens elsewhere, Workspace.saveWorkspace() at src/ctrmap/Workspace.java:618 (prefs.put/prefs.flush/current.savePersist()), called from SetupWizard.java:609 and CtrmapMainframe.java:796. Two qualifications to the census claim, which lower it 

## L.2 storeFile takes a byte[] with no null refusal while five producers in the tree answer null on failure

`src/ctrmap/formats/containers/AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:291` - **api-trap** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `public void storeFile(int num, byte[] data)` opens with `ctrmap.util.Bytes.getPadding(getOffset(num), data.length)` - no null test anywhere in the body. Producers that answer null on failure and are passed straight into it: `GRPropData.assemblePropData()` (GRPropData.java:92-97, `catch (IOException ex) { ...log...; return null; }`) at GRPropData.java:40 and :79; `GFLPawnScript.getScriptBytes()` (GFLPawnScript.java:168-176) at Zone.java:108, on every zone store; `ZoneHeader.assembleData()` (ZoneHeader.java:158) at Zone.java:100; `Tilemap.assembleTilemap()` (Tilemap.java:103) at TileMapPanel.java:430/641/647, GeoEditForm.java:655 and Builder.java:524; `ResourceAccess.getByteArray` at Builder.java:530 and ExtrasPanel.java:169.

**What the user gets.** Where the container is padded (GR, GR.java:57-59) the caller gets a NullPointerException from inside `getPadding` with no message; where it is not (ZO, ZO.java:43-45) `checkStoreStatus(num, null)` is `!Arrays.equals(null, getFile(num))`, so if the existing subfile is also unreadable the two nulls compare equal and storeFile returns having written nothing at all - a save that reports success over a write that did not happen. Either outcome replaces the named refusal storeFile was rewritten to give ("could not write subfile N of X") with silence or a bare NPE.

**Fix.** Refuse at the parameter: `if (data == null) throw new IllegalStateException("nothing to store into subfile " + num + " of " + f.getName())` is the cheap version, but the class-closing one is to stop the producers answering null - assemblePropData/getScriptBytes/assembleData/assembleTilemap should throw the way ZoneEntities.assembleData already does, so there is no null to pass.

**Verified.** The API hole is literally in the source, but the census overstates the harm. CONFIRMED text: AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:291-293 `public void storeFile(int num, byte[] data)` dereferences `data.length` inside `Bytes.getPadding(getOffset(num), data.length)` with no null test in the body; AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:260-262 `checkStoreStatus` is `!Arrays.equals(data, getFile(num))` and getFile answers null on a read failure (AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:224-232), so a null/null pair compares equal and line 302-304 returns having written nothing. The named producers do return null and are passed in unchecked: GRPropData.java:96 -> :40 and :79; GFLPawnScript.java:175 -> Zone.java:108; ZoneHeader.java:212 -> Zone.java:100; Tilemap.java:131 -> TileMapPanel.java:430/641/647, GeoEditForm.java:655, Builder.java:524; ResourceAccess.java:21 -> Builder.java:530, ExtrasPanel.java:169. REFUTED part: no null-producing branch is reachable today. Every one of those returns sits in a catch(IOException) around a ByteArrayOutputStream (LittleEndianDataOutputStream.java:10-13 wraps DataOutputStream over BAOS), none of the four has a deliberate `throw new IOException`, and GRProp.write swallows its own IOException (GRProp.java:105-107) so assemblePropData's catch can only fire from `dos.writeInt(props.size())`. ResourceAccess.getByteArray NPEs at `in.available()` on a missing resource rat

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/TilePalette.java:84` - **api-trap** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `public final int[] tuple;` - final protects the reference, not the four bytes, and those bytes are what PaintedTiles.java:61-64 writes into the region's movement map (`out[off] = (byte) tuple[0];`). Same for `public final String[] matHints` (:91). Separately, brushes() (:157) hand-lists twenty of the enum's own constants.

A brush added to the enum is silently missing from the painter until someone remembers the second list - the swatch simply is not there, with no error.

**Fix.** Hand out a copy (`int[] tuple()` returning tuple.clone()) or keep the four bytes as four fields, so the constants cannot be edited at all; and give the enum a `brush` flag so brushes() is derived from values() rather than maintained beside it.

No assignment, sort, or escape of either reference exists, so the claimed "change what GRASS means, next Apply writes the bytes" path has no first link; it is speculation. It is also the house idiom, not a TilePalette peculiarity: 57 `public final ...[]` fields exist outside src/ctrmap/tests (AreaEnv.java:105, DressUpIndex.java:87-173, ZoneLimitPatch.java:68 and so on), so singling this one out is a style opinion. (2) CONFIRMED: TilePalette.java:157-161 `public static TilePalette[] brushes() { return new TilePalette[]{GRASS, ... `src/ctrmap/formats/propdata/ADPropRegistry.java:211` - **correctness** - effort: small

But equals is the change detector for the registry editor's save - `if (!e2.equals(e))` decides whether the edit is kept - so the first caller that passes null or a different type gets "unchanged" and their edit is discarded without a word, and the entry can never be used in a HashSet or as a map key.

So `entry.equals(null)` and `entry.equals("x")` both answer true today — a straight violation of the Object.equals contract (equals(null) must be false), and the fall-through returns the DANGEROUS answer for a change detector: "equal" means "unchanged" means "discard the edit". There is no hashCode anywhere in the file (grep for hashCode in ADPropRegistry.java returns nothing), so equals is overridden without it — the second half of the same contract broken. The u2 omission is also real: lines 206-209 compare reference, model, eventScr1, eventScr2, u0, isOmnipresentAnimated, u1, omnipresentAnimations, evtAnimations1, evtAnimations2 and stop — u2 (the 0x24 bytes declared at line 138 and round-tripped by read at line 158 / write at line 198) is not in the list. ## L.5 NPC drag arithmetic divides by zero at tiles 0 and 1 and poisons the accumulator permanently

`src/ctrmap/formats/zone/ZoneEntities.java:401` - **correctness** - effort: small

**What the code does.** setX: `floatMotionRemainder = 18f - (val % ((xTile - 1) * 18f));` and in the else branch `floatMotionRemainder = val % (xTile * 18f);`. At xTile == 1 the first is `val % 0f` = NaN; at xTile == 0 the second is `val % 0f` = NaN. Once NaN, every later `floatMotionRemainder += ...` stays NaN and `if (floatMotionRemainder >= 18f)` is false forever. setZ (line 475) is the same code for yTile.

**What the user gets.** Drag an NPC across the top-left corner of a map once and its sub-tile accumulator is dead for the rest of the session - the correction that nudges it to the adjacent tile never fires again, so the NPC snaps differently from every other NPC and the user cannot tell why.

**Fix.** Guard the divisor (or drop the accumulator: xTile is already recomputed by Math.round at the top of both methods, which is what actually moves the NPC). Either way a guard that drags through tile 0 and asserts the remainder stays a number.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed and the path is live, but the claimed harm is wrong. ZoneEntities.java:401 is literally `floatMotionRemainder = 18f - (val % ((xTile - 1) * 18f));` guarded by `if (val < xTile * 18f)`, and :413 is `floatMotionRemainder = val % (xTile * 18f);` in the else branch; :475/:487 are the same pair for yTile in setZ. Java float `%` by 0f is NaN, so xTile==1 poisons the first branch and xTile==0 the second. Reachable with no clamp anywhere: NPC defaults are `xTile = 0` (:270) and `floatMotionOrigin = -1` (:278), the gizmo drag calls it at H3DRenderingPanel.java:236 `navi.target.setX(navi.target.getX() + ...)` on the NPC bound by NPCEditForm.java:324 `navi.follow(e.npcs.get(index))`, and CM3DNavigator.synchronizeModel (:57-64) passes the raw float through. REFUTED parts: (a) not permanent - the `xTile != floatMotionOrigin` branches ASSIGN rather than `+=`, so the NaN is overwritten with a real number as soon as the NPC reaches tile 2 or beyond; (b) not cross-NPC - floatMotionOrigin/floatMotionRemainder are instance fields (:278-279), so no other NPC "snaps differently"; (c) no bad bytes - xTile is already fixed by `Math.round(val / 18f)` at :397 before the accumulator runs, setYFromColl is handed `xTile * 18f` (:423) so no NaN altitude, write() (:356-358) never serialises floatMotionRemainder, and equals() (:365-375) ignores it. ## L.6 The camera walker reads its key set across threads with no synchronisation

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/CM3DInputManager.java:29` - **correctness** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `private ArrayList<Integer> keycodes = new ArrayList<>();` is mutated on the EDT by keyPressed (133) and keyReleased (`while (keycodes.contains(e.getKeyCode())) { keycodes.remove((Integer) e.getKeyCode()); }`, 215-217) and read by the "camera-walk" thread in walking() (172-175) and step() (189-206). Nothing is synchronized or volatile; `speed` (28) is shared the same way. Only startWalking() is synchronized, and it guards the thread field, not the set.

**What the user gets.** The walker has no happens-before with the release, so it can go on stepping the camera after the key is up - the very behaviour the method's javadoc says was fixed ("A key press whose release never arrives ... left one running for ever") - and a remove racing a contains can throw out of the walker, killing movement silently until the next keypress.

**Fix.** A concurrent structure the walker can trust - a synchronized set, or an AtomicInteger bitmask of held directions - and a volatile speed. The daemon flag stops the leak; it does not make the read correct.

**Verified.** Verified line by line in src/ctrmap/humaninterface/CM3DInputManager.java. Line 29 `private ArrayList<Integer> keycodes = new ArrayList<>();` and line 28 `private float speed = 10f;` are plain fields — no volatile, no concurrent wrapper. EDT writers: line 133 `keycodes.add(e.getKeyCode());`; lines 215-217 `while (keycodes.contains(e.getKeyCode())) { keycodes.remove((Integer) e.getKeyCode()); }`; line 110 `speed = Math.max(-e.getWheelRotation() + speed, 2f);`. Other-thread readers: the "camera-walk" Runnable started at line 168 reads the set at 173-174 (walking()) and 189-206 (step(), which also reads `speed` at 190-206). The only synchronized member is line 147 `private synchronized void startWalking()`, and the walker body (151-165) never acquires that monitor, so it creates no happens-before for anything after the first press; keyReleased (214-218) takes no lock and writes no volatile at all, so the release has no edge with the walker (the Thread.sleep at line 157 has no memory-consistency effect). Two consequences read straight out of the source: (1) `walking()` is the ONLY stop condition — grepping all of src/ finds no `walker.interrupt()`; the sole interrupt hits are `Thread.currentThread().interrupt()` at CM3DInputManager.java:162, LoadingDialog.java:81, Updater.java:492 and :599 — so the interrupt path the javadoc at 160-163 leans on is never triggered and the unsynchroni

## L.7 TerrainHarvester's "dominant cliff material" is the first one it happens to meet

`src/ctrmap/tools/TerrainHarvester.java:240` - **correctness** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `if (++rockBest[ty][tx] > 0 && rockMaterial[ty][tx] == null) { rockMaterial[ty][tx] = mat; }` - rockBest is incremented and then compared to 0, which is always true after an increment, and it is read nowhere else in the file (grep: declared 200, touched only at 240). The second clause makes the assignment first-wins. The comment three lines above states the opposite rule: "whichever cliff material contributes most triangles here is what this tile looks like, and matching that is the difference between a grass hillside and a desert mesa dropped into the middle of a meadow". The result travels to cutAll/cutRuns through a mutable static field, `static String[][] rockMaterial` (line 195), so the call order rockDensity-then-cut is load-bearing and nothing enforces it.

**What the user gets.** The material column written into the kit is whichever cliff material came first in mesh order, not the dominant one - by the author's own account, the difference between a matching hillside and a desert mesa in a meadow. rockBest is dead weight that makes the line read as if the max rule were implemented.

**Fix.** Compare before assigning (`if (++rockBest[ty][tx] > best) ...` with a real per-tile maximum) or delete rockBest and say first-wins. Either way return the grid from rockDensity instead of leaving it in a static, so a caller cannot read last region's answer.

**Verified.** Confirmed at the line. src/ctrmap/tools/TerrainHarvester.java:240 reads `if (++rockBest[ty][tx] > 0 && rockMaterial[ty][tx] == null) { rockMaterial[ty][tx] = mat; }`. rockBest is `int[][]` allocated at :200 and only ever incremented, so `++rockBest[...] > 0` is unconditionally true (it is >= 1 after every increment); the effective predicate is `rockMaterial[ty][tx] == null`, i.e. FIRST cliff material in mesh order wins, never the most numerous. `grep -rn rockBest src` returns exactly two hits, :200 and :240 - it is written and never read, so no max rule exists anywhere. The comment immediately above (:236-239) states the opposite contract: "whichever cliff material contributes most triangles here is what this tile looks like". The result is not corrected downstream: cutAll at :285 takes `if (mat == null && rockMaterial[y][x] != null)` (first non-null tile, row-major) and cutRuns at :354 does the same along the run, so both consumers compound first-wins rather than fix it, and `mat` is written out as the last field of every cut row (:304-:308) and as the `material` column of every run row (:391-:394). NOT GUARDED: `grep -rn TerrainHarvester` over the whole tree hits only the file itself and DuplicateWorkTest.java:91 (its UNREACHED entry); no suite constructs or calls rockDensity. The near-identical dominance defect IS guarded for the sibling tool - HarvesterGuardsTest.java:144 `

`src/ctrmap/ZoneManager.java:150` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

Nothing in renameZoneAction probes for a free name slot before promising or before writing, so consent is taken on a statement the code can break. The stale class javadoc at ZoneManager.java:22 ("a NEW name line is appended and the zone repointed to it") is also real - nothing appends; findFreeBlankSlot (ZoneManager.java:172-189) reuses a blank in-bounds slot and the comment at ZoneManager.java:141-144 explains that appending would break the 356 place-id bound.
