## L.9 Both GARC writers hard-code a 0x1C header and a one-bit FATB while the reader honours what the file declares

`src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:477` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** parse() finds FATO with `in.seek(garcLength)` - the header length read from the file (line 71). writeRepacked instead walks a fixed 0x1C (16 header bytes copied verbatim at 467-468, then three ints, then FATO at 483) and copies the original's declared header length inside those 16 bytes; GarcRebuilder.java:32 reads `int fatoPos = i32(head, 4)` and then writes the same fixed 0x1C. parse() also expands one FATO slot into as many entries as it has flag bits (lines 98-121), while writeRepacked reads the old FATB as exactly four ints per entry (505-510) - a shape it cannot reproduce. MEASURED on the user's own dump with a FATO/FATB scan: 23 of 298 retail ORAS archives have slots with flags 445 or 447 (7 and 9 bits), including a/0/2/0 - one index away from MOVE_MODELS (a/0/2/1), which IS moddable.

**What the user gets.** No ORAS archive this editor opens today is one of the 23, so nothing is broken now. But the writer silently cannot represent what the reader accepts, and nothing refuses the mismatch: the day an ArchiveType names one of those paths (or a Gen 7 dump with a 0x24 v6 header arrives), the pack writes FATO at the wrong offset and the FATB from misaligned bytes, and the archive is destroyed with a success dialog. GARC.length (FATO slots) and getEntryCount() (FATB entries) are used interchangeably across the tree for the same reason.

**Fix.** Refuse in parse() when the declared header length is not the one the writer emits, and when any FATO slot has more than one flag bit - an archive this editor cannot write back is one it must not open.

**Verified.** The source says exactly what is claimed, and I re-measured the dump independently. READER: src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:71+80 read the declared header length and seek to it (`int garcLength = ...; in.seek(garcLength);`), and :104 expands one FATO slot into one entry per set flag bit (`for (int bit = 0; bit < 32; bit++) if ((flags & (1 << bit)) > 0)`); the version read at :73 is never checked, and :99 `if (flags != 1)` only builds an unused folder-name string. WRITER: GARC.java:465-468 copies a fixed 16-byte header (`byte[] buf = new byte[16]; old.read(buf); dos.write(buf);`), then two placeholder ints, `old.skip(8)` and one readInt, so both streams are hard-positioned at 0x1C when the FATO magic is read at :475 and written at :483 - the declared header length inside those 16 copied bytes is never honoured. GARC.java:502-510 then reads the old FATB as exactly four ints per entry for `oldEntryCount` entries, a shape that cannot represent a multi-bit slot (4 + bits*12). GarcRebuilder.java:32 is the same: it computes `int fatoPos = i32(head, 4)` for the template, then at :59-64 writes head verbatim + 12 bytes, putting FATO at 0x1C regardless, and :78 stamps `flags0` on every slot. MY OWN SCAN of <session folder>/RomFS/000400000011C400 reproduces the census number exactly: 298 GARCs, 23 with a slot whose flags != 1 (a/0/1/9, a/0/2/0, a/0/2/4, a/

## L.10 The party table silently clamps out-of-range values and carries five more raw copies of bounds the same file argues against

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/TrainerEditDialog.java:330` - **data-integrity** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** setValueAt: `case 0: m.species = Math.max(1, Math.min(721, val)); break;` and the same shape for form (31), level (100), ivByte (255), genderAbility (0x32), heldItem (775) and moves (621), with `catch (NumberFormatException ignore) {}` around the lot; the class spinner is `new SpinnerNumberModel(t.classId, 0, 279, 1)` at line 147. The file's own comment at lines 80-88 makes the opposite case for the trainer id: "THE SHARED BOUND, not a fifth copy of the number... Four other places already agree on 949 ... this was the only outlier, so it now names the same constant instead of repeating the digits."

**What the user gets.** Typing 900 into a species cell writes 721 into the party record without a word, so the saved trainer is not the one the user typed; and the next game (or the next form) gets its own copy of these seven numbers to drift from, which is the defect the trainer-id comment describes.

**Fix.** Name the bounds once beside the record layout (TrainerEntry / PokeData already own the tables these come from) and refuse a value outside them in words rather than clamping - the user typed something the format cannot hold and should be told so.

**Verified.** Partly real, but the headline harm is refuted and what survives is the duplication half only. REFUTED — the claimed harm cannot happen. "Typing 900 into a species cell" is impossible: TrainerEditDialog.java:298-301 `isCellEditable` returns `c == 2 || c == 3 || c == 4 || c == 5`, so species(0), item(6) and moves(8-11) are NOT typeable. PokePickers.java:61-62 states the contract outright — "The mapped columns should be marked non-editable so the picker (not a text editor) opens" — and PokePickers.java:84-88 only writes back `picked` chosen from its own list (`result[0] = visibleIds.get(vi)`, PokePickers.java:181-183 bounds that list by `speciesCount()`). So four of the seven clamps the finding lists (721, 775, 621 x1) are only reachable with an already-valid id. REFUTED — "without a word" is false for the four clamps that ARE reachable (form/level/IV/G-A, lines 331-334). TrainerEditDialog.java:337 calls `fireTableRowsUpdated(r, r)` immediately after the clamp, so a user who types 150 into Level sees the cell redraw as 100 before saving, and Save (lines 213-224) writes exactly the bytes the table is showing. That is not a dialog reporting success over a write that did not happen — the write matches the display. The empty `catch (NumberFormatException ignore) {}` at TrainerEditDialog.java:339 is already inside the ratcheted 55 empty catches under SilentCatchTest, so it is not a new

## L.11 The harvester's temp cut cache is keyed on region index alone and never cleaned, so a second dump reuses the first one's bytes

`src/ctrmap/tools/BuildingHarvester.java:168` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `File tmp = new File(tmpDir, "reg_" + c.region); if (!tmp.exists()) { try (FileOutputStream fo = ...) { fo.write(gr.getDecompressedEntry(c.region)); } }` where tmpDir is `new File(System.getProperty("java.io.tmpdir"), "ctrmap_harvest")` (line 154). The name carries the region index and nothing about WHICH dump it came from, the folder is never deleted, and the run ends without clearing it.

**What the user gets.** Run the harvester against a second dump - a different region, a modified copy, a partially repacked folder - and every region already cached is cut from the PREVIOUS dump's bytes while the run reports the new one. The output is the committed building catalogue the editor cuts a user's geometry with, so the wrong donor bytes become tile boxes shipped in oras_buildings_auto.tsv, and nothing in the run says a cached file was used. This project has already paid once for a stale pack (zone 536).

**Fix.** Key the cache on the dump as well as the region (the archive's path plus its size/mtime, or a digest) and delete the folder at the end of a run - or drop the cache entirely and cut from the GARC each time, which is what correctness costs here.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed, and I could not find a caller or suite that closes it. BuildingHarvester.java:154-155 `File tmpDir = new File(System.getProperty("java.io.tmpdir"), "ctrmap_harvest"); tmpDir.mkdirs();` — a fixed, shared, persistent directory; :168-173 `File tmp = new File(tmpDir, "reg_" + c.region); if (!tmp.exists()) { try (FileOutputStream fo = new FileOutputStream(tmp)) { fo.write(gr.getDecompressedEntry(c.region)); } }` then :174 `GR reg = new GR(tmp, harvested);`. The cache key is the region INDEX only — no dump identity, no length or digest check, no timestamp. `grep -rn "ctrmap_harvest|deleteOnExit|reg_"` over src/ returns only lines 154/155/159/168 in this file: nothing deletes tmpDir or the reg_ files, at start or at exit, and main() ends at :226 with `System.out.println("wrote " + rows.size() + ...)` having said nothing about cached files. The reuse is silent both ways: the success path never reports a cache hit, and a bad cached file lands in the swallow at :207-209 `catch (Exception ex) { dropped++; }`, indistinguishable from a legitimately rejected cut. That also makes a second, single-dump route reachable: a run killed inside the write at :170-172 leaves the file EXISTING but short, and the next run takes it because `tmp.exists()` is true. Reachability is real — BuildingHarvester.main is invoked by a suite as well as by hand: HarvesterGuardsT

## L.12 Vault.list() silently drops one of two vaults sealed in the same millisecond, and that is the only way VaultUi finds a manifest

`src/ctrmap/vault/Vault.java:639` - **data-integrity** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `Map<Long, VaultManifest> byTime = new LinkedHashMap<>(); ... byTime.put(m.sealedAt, m); ... out.addAll(byTime.values());` followed immediately by an explicit `Collections.sort(out, ...)` on the same field - so the map buys nothing and costs a collision. sealedAt also defaults to 0 for any manifest missing that line, which collapses every such entry onto one key. VaultUi.read(File entry) then finds a manifest by scanning this list (`for (VaultManifest m : Vault.list()) if (m.entryDir.equals(entry)) return m;`, VaultUi.java:192-199) even though VaultManifest.read(entry) is package-private and one call away.

**What the user gets.** A vault that loses the key collision disappears from every list and, through VaultUi.read, from restoreEverything - which then says "There is no backup of this game to restore from" about a sealed, verified copy sitting on disk. Two manifests with no sealedAt line is enough to trigger it without any clock coincidence.

**Fix.** Drop the map - collect into the list and sort, which is what the code already does one line later. And have VaultUi.read call VaultManifest.read(entry) directly, so finding one entry stops depending on every other entry on the machine being distinct.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed, and the harm chain is reachable end to end. Vault.java:645-656 builds `Map<Long, VaultManifest> byTime = new LinkedHashMap<>();`, does `byTime.put(m.sealedAt, m);` per entry dir, then `out.addAll(byTime.values());` and IMMEDIATELY re-sorts with `Collections.sort(out, ... Long.compare(b.sealedAt, a.sealedAt))` - so the map cannot be there for ordering; its only effect is that two entries with equal sealedAt collapse to one, silently. sealedAt is not a unique key by construction: Vault.java:235 sets `man.sealedAt = System.currentTimeMillis();` at the START of a seal (before the copy), while the entry's identity is its directory (Vault.java:134-138 `entryDir` = titleId or "game-<TYPE>"), so a root legitimately holds several entries (the suite's own root accumulates 000400000011C400, ...C401, ...C402) and nothing ties their sealedAt values apart. The lookup is the only one: VaultUi.java:192-199 `static VaultManifest read(File entry) { for (VaultManifest m : Vault.list()) if (m.entryDir != null && m.entryDir.equals(entry)) return m; } return null;`, called at VaultUi.java:144 by restoreEverything, which on null reports "There is no backup of this game to restore from." (VaultUi.java:148). That contradicts its own caller: CtrmapMainframe.java:2402 has already gated on `if (!ctrmap.vault.Vault.isSealed(entry))` (manifest file present) before call

## L.13 Help > Quick start guide looks in the same place twice and never where package.ps1 puts the guide

`src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:951` - **dead-code** - effort: small

**What the code does.** quickStartAction: `File guide = new File("QUICKSTART.md"); if (!guide.isFile()) { guide = new File(System.getProperty("user.dir"), "QUICKSTART.md"); }`. A relative java.io.File resolves against the current user directory, which IS System.getProperty("user.dir"), so the fallback tests the identical path - it can never succeed when the first test failed. Neither branch asks where the program is installed, although this same file uses ctrmap.update.Updater.installDir() three times at lines 787-790, and Updater.installDir() (Updater.java:163) returns the bundle root or the jar's folder. package.ps1 copies QUICKSTART.md to the portable stage root (line 86) and to dist\app\CTRMap-F5\ (line 125) - beside the program, not into whatever folder happens to be the CWD.

**What the user gets.** The door that OUTSTANDING item D just added for first-run users - "the quick start guide that has shipped beside the program with no door to it" - opens onto an error dialog for anyone whose working directory is not the install folder, which is the normal case for a Start-menu or desktop shortcut into the Windows bundle. The dead second check makes the code read as if two locations were tried.

**Fix.** Look in Updater.installDir() first and fall back to the CWD, and delete the duplicate test. The error message should name both places it looked, so a miss is diagnosable.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED, with the harm narrower than claimed. src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:951-954 reads exactly as reported: `File guide = new File("QUICKSTART.md"); if (!guide.isFile()) { guide = new File(System.getProperty("user.dir"), "QUICKSTART.md"); }`. A relative java.io.File resolves against the process working directory, which is what user.dir is initialised from, and nothing in src/ ever calls System.setProperty("user.dir") (grep over src/ returns nothing), so the two abstract paths are the same file and the second isFile() can only be false when the first was - the fallback at :953 is unreachable-as-a-second-location, i.e. dead. The lookup never asks where the program is installed, although ctrmap.update.Updater.installDir() (src/ctrmap/update/Updater.java:163) is already used from this very file at :776 and :799 and returns precisely the folder package.ps1 puts the guide in: the bundle root for the jpackage image (`Copy-Item README.md, LICENSE, QUICKSTART.md "dist\app\CTRMap-F5\"`, package.ps1:125, and installDir() returns appImageRoot(), Updater.java:164-167) and the jar's own folder for the portable zip (`Copy-Item run.bat, run.sh, README.md, LICENSE, QUICKSTART.md "$stage\"`, package.ps1:86, and installDir() returns self().getParentFile(), Updater.java:168-171). The error text at :956-957 then asserts "QUICKSTART.md

## L.15 Dead members whose only reachable behaviour is a crash or a comment

`src/ctrmap/formats/h3d/PICACommandReader.java:21` - **dead-code** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `float[] lookUpTable = new float[256];` and `lutIndex` are written at lines 47 and 59 (`lookUpTable[lutIndex++] = commands[id];`) and read NOWHERE in the tree — so the only possible effect of those two statements is an ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException when a LUT upload exceeds 256 entries (extraParameters is 11 bits wide, line 36); and the assignment widens a long command word to float as a NUMBER, discarding the IEEE bit pattern that toFloat() exists three lines away to preserve. Also unreferenced anywhere: getTexUnit0/1/2BorderColor (463/520/578 — each would throw IllegalArgumentException for any channel >= 0x80, because `new Color((byte)(rgba & 0xff), ...)` sign-extends), getTexUnit1/2Size, getTexUnit1/2Address, getVSHBooleanUniforms, the no-arg getVSHTotalAttributes and getVSHAttributesBufferAddress; OhanaMeshUtils.getColorFloat (82, same Color sign-extension bug), OhanaMeshUtils.optimizedMesh (108) and optimizerLookBack (106) — both referenced only from the commented-out C# block at 120-162; H3DModel.adjustBoneVerticesToMatrix (418, javadoc "Deprecated. Replaced by fixed rigid skinning instead."), transformVec3f (501), verticesCount (56), and doVBOUpdate (641) which is written at 460 and never read; BchModelVerifier.java:102-103, two consecutive `cur=pp;` assignments both overwritten at 106, one labelled "// placeholder".

**What the user gets.** Nobody is bitten today, which is the point: it reads as live code. Anyone wiring up the border colours or the LUT gets a method that throws on ordinary retail data, and the two-copy string/colour bugs keep being copied because the broken versions look like the house style.

**Fix.** Delete them. DuplicateWorkTest already walks references to a fixed point for whole classes — the same walk at member granularity would refuse the next one instead of finding it in a census.

**Verified.** Every named line checks out; I read each one and grepped the whole single source root (no reflection reaches them — the only getDeclaredMethod calls in src/ are test hooks on Swing forms). PICACommandReader.java:21-22 `float[] lookUpTable = new float[256]; int lutIndex;` are written at :47 and :59 (`lookUpTable[lutIndex++] = commands[id];`) and appear NOWHERE else in the tree (grep for lookUpTable|lutIndex returns exactly those five lines, the fifth being the `lutIndex = 0;` reset at :70) — a write-only array, and the assignment is a numeric widening of a long command word while the sibling branch three lines up at :45 uses `toFloat((int) commands[id])` to preserve the IEEE pattern. Unreferenced members confirmed by grep returning only the declaration: getTexUnit0/1/2BorderColor (:463/:520/:578), getTexUnit1Size (:534), getTexUnit2Size (:592), getTexUnit1Address (:493), getTexUnit2Address (:551), getVSHBooleanUniforms (:178), and the no-arg getVSHTotalAttributes (:107) / getVSHAttributesBufferAddress (:131) — only the (int bufferIndex) overloads at :160/:169 have callers (H3DModel.java:254,256). The three border-colour bodies really do read `new Color((byte) (rgba & 0xff), (byte) ((rgba >> 8) & 0xff), ...)`, which binds Color(int,int,int,int) on sign-extended bytes, so any channel >= 0x80 throws IllegalArgumentException; OhanaMeshUtils.java:82-89 getColorFloat repeats it verbat

## L.16 ResizeableMatrix.resizeMatrix can only throw, and nothing calls it

`src/ctrmap/formats/mapmatrix/MapMatrix.java:357` - **dead-code** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `for (int j = l.size(); j >= 0; j--) { if (j >= newHeight) { l.remove(j); } }` - j starts AT l.size(), so the first iteration calls l.remove(l.size()) whenever newHeight <= l.size(), which is every shrink and every no-op resize. The column loop above it is written correctly (`for (int i = list.size() - 1; ...)`). Zero callers: MatrixEditForm uses addColumn/addRow/removeColumns/removeRows instead.

**What the user gets.** A public resize method on the type that holds the map's region grid throws IndexOutOfBoundsException for every input except growth. The next caller - the obvious one to reach for when resizing a matrix - gets a crash mid-edit, and the correct off-by-one is sitting eight lines above it.

**Fix.** Delete it (nothing reaches it) or fix the bound to l.size() - 1 and cover it. removeColumns/removeRows have the matching hazard when count exceeds the current size.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED. src/ctrmap/formats/mapmatrix/MapMatrix.java:357 reads `for (int j = l.size(); j >= 0; j--) { if (j >= newHeight) { l.remove(j); } }` — j starts at l.size(), so whenever newHeight <= l.size() the first iteration calls ArrayList.remove(l.size()) and throws IndexOutOfBoundsException; that is every shrink, every same-height resize, and every width-only resize. The column loop eight lines above at MapMatrix.java:350 is written correctly (`for (int i = list.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--)`), which also means it has already removed columns from the live grid before the throw, leaving a half-resized matrix for any caller that catches. Only a strict height growth (newHeight > l.size()) reaches ensureSize at MapMatrix.java:365-368, so the title's "can only throw" is a mild overstatement while the evidence body is exact. Zero callers: a repo-wide grep for `resizeMatrix` returns exactly one source hit, the declaration at MapMatrix.java:349 (plus the compiled .class in build/); src/ctrmap/humaninterface/MatrixEditForm.java resizes via the sibling API instead — mm.ids.addColumn()/mm.zones.addColumns(4) at lines 870,894, addRow()/addRows(4) at 904-909, removeRows(4)/removeColumns(4) at 920,931.

## L.17 The move-model name cache promises invalidation on repack and nothing invalidates it

`src/ctrmap/formats/npcreg/MoveModelPool.java:49` - **dead-code** - effort: small

**What the code does.** invalidate() ("Drop the cache (e.g. when the archives were repacked)", l.48-52) is called from no production code — grep over src finds callers only in HandedGameTest.java:708/716. The cache is otherwise dropped only when a different GameFiles instance is handed in (`if (names != null && loadedFrom == files) return;` l.64), and a pack does not replace the session object the application hands around.

**What the user gets.** The class comment tells a reader the cache is dropped when the archives are repacked; it is not. After a pack that changed a MoveModels entry, the model picker (ModelPicker.java:120-125) still lists the pre-pack BCH names, and the user picks a model by a name the workspace no longer holds.

**Fix.** Call invalidate() from the pack completion path, or key the cache on something a pack changes so a stale entry cannot be served. A dropper nobody drops is the same kind of decoration as a convention nobody follows.

**Verified.** Confirmed at the lines claimed, with one correction to the trigger. (1) src/ctrmap/formats/npcreg/MoveModelPool.java:48-52 is `/** Drop the cache (e.g. when the archives were repacked). */ public static synchronized void invalidate()`, and a repo-wide grep for `MoveModelPool.invalidate` returns only src/ctrmap/tests/HandedGameTest.java:708 and :723 — no production caller. (2) The only other drop is identity-keyed: MoveModelPool.java:64 `if (names != null && loadedFrom == files) return;`, and a pack does not replace the session — WorkspaceSession.packArchives is an instance method that only swaps GARC handles (reloadGARC at :980-986), so `loadedFrom == files` still holds afterwards. (3) The oversight is visible at the pack site itself: WorkspaceSession.java:990 calls `ctrmap.formats.propdata.PropDatabase.invalidate()` ("the session prop database was built from the pre-pack GARCs; drop it so the next palette use rebuilds"), and the twin cache with the identical doc contract is not dropped there. (4) The staleness is reachable in one session: the Builder lets the user select MOVE_MODELS (Builder.java:675 `currentGARC = ArchiveType.values()[index]`) and import over an entry — Builder.java:426/447/653 `persistentContainerReference.storeFile(index, output)` writes the staged workspace file that MoveModelPool.java:73 reads — and Builder.java:558-559 packs and reloads the archive after

## L.21 The tile painter's textured terrain preview was built and never wired to anything

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/TerrainTextures.java:27` - **dead-code** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** `public static TerrainTextures build(byte[] donorModel, List<H3DTexture> worldTextures)` (line 27) has zero call sites in all of src/ - the only reference to the class from the application is the unrelated static `TerrainTextures.materialImage(...)` at CtrmapMainframe.java:2905. Its two instance accessors, `image(TilePalette)` (line 61) and `any()` (line 65), likewise have zero call sites, so the `images` map (line 24) is filled by nobody and read by nobody. The class javadoc promises "so the tile painter can show a top-down 'in-game' textured preview (grass looks like grass, water like water) instead of flat swatches", and TileMapPanel.getWorldTextures()'s javadoc (TileMapPanel.java:70) says it exists "for the tile painter's textured preview" - but PaintForm's brush renderer paints flat swatches: `g.setColor(t.color()); g.fillRect(x, y, 13, 13);` (PaintForm.java:176-177), and the world textures it does fetch (PaintForm.java:214) go only to BuildingPaletteDialog.

**What the user gets.** The user paints terrain from a list of flat colour chips; the feature that would show them what grass/water/sand actually look like exists in the tree, is reachable from nothing, and reads as shipped to anyone opening the file. DuplicateWorkTest cannot see it because the class name is mentioned for a different static.

## L.30 Bytes.getTrimmedArray has no production caller and two pinned defects

`src/ctrmap/util/Bytes.java:122` - **dead-code** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `for (int i = in.length - 1; i > 0; i--)` never examines index 0, and `int finalIndex = i + (4 - (i % 4))` asks for four bytes past an already-aligned i. UtilsTest pins both as current behaviour: "a value in slot 0 ALONE also trims to nothing - the scan never looks at index 0" and a try/catch asserting ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException for `{0,0,0,0,9}`. Grepping the tree, the only callers are those five assertions - no production code calls it.

**What the user gets.** A public helper in the class the format layer is explicitly invited to use freely returns an empty array for {1,0,0,0} and throws for any non-multiple-of-four buffer ending in data. It is pinned, not refused, so the trap is documented where only a test reader will find it - and the first caller to trim a subfile with it writes a zero-length payload or crashes.

**Fix.** Delete it. Nothing calls it, and deletion closes both pinned defects and the trap at once - a refusal at the point of action (there is no method to call) rather than a suite describing the wreckage.

**Verified.** CONFIRMED, with one correction to the arithmetic wording. src/ctrmap/util/Bytes.java:123 reads `for (int i = in.length - 1; i > 0; i--)` — the condition is `i > 0`, so index 0 is never tested, and line 131 `return new byte[0];` is what {1,0,0,0} therefore gets. Line 125 `int finalIndex = i + (4 - (i % 4));` gives 8 for i=4, and line 127 `System.arraycopy(in, 0, ret, 0, finalIndex);` then reads 8 bytes out of a 5-byte input, so {0,0,0,0,9} throws ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException. (Nuance the census got slightly wrong: 8 IS the correct word-rounded length for 5 bytes; the defect is that the copy LENGTH is taken from the rounded size instead of `Math.min(finalIndex, in.length)`, so it overruns the source rather than zero-padding. The throw is real either way.) Dead-code half also holds: a tree-wide `grep -rn getTrimmedArray` returns only src/ctrmap/util/Bytes.java:122 and five assertions in src/ctrmap/tests/UtilsTest.java (lines 154-164) — no production caller anywhere in src/. The javadoc claim is literal too: Bytes.java:22 says "it is why the format layer may use this class freely", and line 121 advertises the method as "The data up to its last non-zero byte, rounded up to a word." Nothing in OUTSTANDING.md (Open is empty; Done and Known limitations never mention it) covers this.

## L.31 The file dialogs' remembered folder is implemented twice and missing from four choosers

`src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:3180` - **duplication** - effort: small

**What the code does.** fileDialogPrefs()/openDialog()/picked() (lines 1319-1341) exist so that "the file dialogs remember the folder they were last in: ONE node, named after this class, with one key per dialog". importMapModelAction ignores them and re-implements the same thing inline: `Preferences prefs = Preferences.userRoot().node(CtrmapMainframe.class.getName()); JFileChooser jfc = new JFileChooser(prefs.get("LAST_DIR", ...)); ... prefs.put("LAST_DIR", chosen.getParent());` - the same node under an un-namespaced key. ## L.32 Two GARC writers; the shrink-capable one flattens every entry's FATB flags to the first entry's

`src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GarcRebuilder.java:78` - **duplication** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** GARC.writeRepacked (GARC.java:436-582) and GarcRebuilder.write (GarcRebuilder.java:24-101) both lay out a whole GARC - header, FATO with the `0xC + n * 4` length and 0xFFFF pad, FATB at n*16, FIMB with the data-length patched last, 4-byte 0xFF entry padding, max-size hint never shrinking. GarcRebuilder's own javadoc says it "mirrors packDirectory's game-validated conventions verbatim", which is the duplication stated as a feature. They have drifted where it matters: writeRepacked carries each entry's own flags through (`fatbe[i].flags = old.readInt();` at :505, re-emitted per entry at :529/:535) and only falls back to `fatbe[0].flags` for entries being appended, while GarcRebuilder reads one flags word from the template (:41 `flags0 = Integer.reverseBytes(in.readInt());`) and writes it for every entry (:78 `dos.writeInt(Integer.reverseBytes(flags0));`). GARC.parse itself treats flags as per-entry and meaningful (`if (flags != 1) folder = ...` at :98).

**What the user gets.** GarcRebuilder is what rewrites ZoneData when a zone is removed (ZoneRemover.java:76 `GarcRebuilder.write(garcFile, garcFile, stored);`), over the user's real archive. Any entry whose FATB flags differ from entry 0's comes back with entry 0's, and there is no verification pass that would say so. More generally, every future correction to the GARC layout - a padding rule, a header field, the max-size hint - has to be made twice, and only one of the two is exercised by the pack path the suites run.

**Fix.** One writer. Extract the layout from GARC.writeRepacked into a method that takes the final list of stored entry bytes plus the per-entry flags and writes the file, then have writeRepacked build that list from its PackPlan and GarcRebuilder build it from its caller's list - so shrinking is a different input to the same writer, not a second writer. Per-entry flags must come from the template's FATB, not from entry 0.

**Verified.** The SOURCE facts are there, but the HARM is refuted by measurement; what survives is bare duplication. CONFIRMED IN SOURCE. Two whole-container writers exist. GARC.writeRepacked (src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:436-582) and GarcRebuilder.write (src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GarcRebuilder.java:24-102) each emit header + FATO (`0xC + n*4`, 0xFFFF pad) + FATB (n*16) + FIMB with the data length patched last, 4-byte 0xFF entry padding, and a max-size hint that never shrinks (GARC.java:473 `Math.max(lastMaxSize, maxlength)` vs GarcRebuilder.java:62 `Math.max(templMax, maxlen)`). GarcRebuilder's own javadoc (:15-17) states the duplication as a feature: "mirrors packDirectory's game-validated conventions verbatim". The flags divergence is real: writeRepacked carries each entry's own word through (GARC.java:503 `fatbe[i].flags = old.readInt();`, re-emitted at :528 and :536) and only falls back to entry 0 for APPENDED entries (:513 `fatbe[i].flags = fatbe[0].flags;`), while GarcRebuilder reads ONE word from the template (:41 `flags0 = Integer.reverseBytes(in.readInt());` — the first FATB record) and writes it for every entry (:78, inside the loop at :76-83). GARC.parse does treat flags as per-entry and meaningful (:99 `if (flags != 1)`, :104 the 32-bit sub-entry loop). Census cited GARC.java:505 for the per-entry read; it is :503. REFUTED — the claimed corruption cannot reach a user's archiv

## L.33 Three winding fixers for one job; the odd one silently mis-shades and its invariant lives in an orphaned comment

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/PaintedFloorMesh.java:220` - **duplication** - effort: small

**What the code does.** fixWindingOut(q,nx,nz) (:220) is exactly fixWindingTowards(q,nx,0f,nz) (:205) - same cross product, same test, same four swaps - and is dead (only caller is the dead cliffQuad). fixWindingUp (:235) is fixWindingTowards(q,0,1,0) MINUS the nrm and ao swaps: `float[] tp = q.pos[1]; ...; float[] tu = q.uv[1]; ...` and nothing else. The note explaining why that is safe - "That helper is only used on quads whose AO is uniform... moving the positions without the shading would light the wall upside down - a fault no geometry check would catch" - sits in an ORPHANED javadoc at :186-196 that precedes fixWindingTowards and therefore documents nothing.

**What the user gets.** Three implementations of "wind this quad to face a direction", so a fix to the winding rule lands on one. fixWindingUp is package-visible to five files; the first caller who hands it a quad with per-corner AO (every cliff and bank quad in the slice has one) gets silently inverted shading on two corners, and by its own comment nothing will catch it.

**Fix.** Delete fixWindingOut, express fixWindingUp as `fixWindingTowards(q, 0f, 1f, 0f)` so one body carries all four swaps, and let the AO come along - there is no quad for which carrying it is wrong. One implementation cannot disagree with itself.

**Verified.** The duplication is plainly in the source, but the claimed harm is half wrong. CONFIRMED: PaintedFloorMesh.java:220 fixWindingOut is byte-for-byte fixWindingTowards(q,nx,0f,nz) from :205 - same a=pos[0],b=pos[2],c=pos[1], same `gx = uy*vz - uz*vy` / `gz = ux*vy - uy*vx`, and the extra gy term at :211 is multiplied by wy=0f, so the dot products are the same float; both swap the identical four slots (pos, uv, nrm, ao at 1<->2). CONFIRMED and WORSE than claimed: fixWindingOut's only caller is PaintedCliffMesh.java:1065 in cliffQuad(:1037); cliffQuad's only caller is :995 in slopedQuad(:993); slopedQuad's only callers are :982-983 in cliffQuads(:945); and `cliffQuads` is referenced NOWHERE in the repo (grep over all file types returns only its own definition). So a ~130-line unreached alternate cliff-face builder ships with the duplicate helper at its base, and DuplicateWorkTest's everyClassIsReachedBySomething (DuplicateWorkTest.java:224) is class-granular - PaintedCliffMesh is reached via roundOutline (:592, called at :370) - so no suite sees the dead method chain. CONFIRMED: the javadoc at PaintedFloorMesh.java:186-196 ("wind so its face points OUT along {@code (nx, nz)}... Unlike fixWindingUp this carries the per-corner AO") is followed by a SECOND javadoc at :197-204 before `static void fixWindingTowards` at :205, so Java attaches only the second; :186-196 documents nothing and

## L.34 TerrainCatalog parses its own table three different ways, and one copy drops the V-band columns

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/TerrainCatalog.java:195` - **duplication** - effort: small

**What the code does.** donors() (:195-240), cliffDonor() (:261-304) and rowNamed() (:342-370) each open openCatalog(), run their own Scanner loop, split on tab, check `f.length < 6` and decode region/area/mesh/material/injectName by hand. Only the first two read the optional bands (`if (f.length > 9) { d.vLip = Float.parseFloat(f[6]); ... d.vFoot = Float.parseFloat(f[9]); }`) - rowNamed, which is what churnDonor() uses (:331), never does. Both copies of the row comment say "[vLip vMid vFoot]" for four columns read at f[6..9].

**What the user gets.** One row format, three readers: a change to the table lands on one of them. Today a CLIFF row gets its measured bands and a LAVA_CHURN row's identical columns are silently ignored, so the same column means something in one row and nothing in the next with no error either way.

**Fix.** One `parseRow(String line)` returning a Donor (or null), and one table read; donors(), cliffDonor() and churnDonor() become filters over it. Then a column can only be read one way.

**Verified.** The structural claim is in the source, verbatim, three times. src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/TerrainCatalog.java:200-233 (donors()), :265-296 (cliffDonor()) and :343-364 (rowNamed()) each open the same openCatalog(), build their own `new Scanner(in, "UTF-8")`, loop hasNextLine/nextLine().trim(), `line.split("\t")`, guard with `if (f.length < 6) { continue; }` and hand-decode f[1]/f[2]/f[3] via Integer.parseInt plus f[4]/f[5] into material/injectName. cliffDonor() and rowNamed() do literally the same job — find one row by its leading keyword and decode it — and differ only in that cliffDonor():286-291 reads the optional bands (`if (f.length > 9) { d.vLip = Float.parseFloat(f[6]); ... d.vFoot = Float.parseFloat(f[9]); }`) while rowNamed() never does; cliffDonor() is rowNamed("CLIFF") plus that block. Both band comments at :221 and :285 do say "[vLip vMid vFoot]", three names for the four columns f[6..9]. No suite refuses this: DuplicateWorkTest's OWNED table (src/ctrmap/tests/DuplicateWorkTest.java:61-107) has no entry for reading the terrain donor table, TerrainImportTest.java:70 only asserts donors() is non-empty, and nothing in src/ctrmap/tests/ mentions vLip, the band columns, or churnDonor. OUTSTANDING.md has no terrain-catalog entry. BUT the census agent's HARM paragraph is wrong on every concrete point and I am correcting it down to low severity: (1) src/ctrmap/resources/oras_

", added zone" : "")`) that exists at no other site; WarpEditForm.java:36 `tgtZone.addItem(i + " - " + LocationNames.getLocName(...))` and MatrixEditForm.java:84 `zoneRefDropdown.addItem(i + " - " + LocationNames.getLocName(...))` spell it in the opposite order. No shared helper exists (grep for zoneLabel/zoneName(/labelFor finds only PaintForm's unrelated JLabel). One part of the census's harm claim is wrong and I discard it: the pickers are not stale, because btnCloneZoneActionPerformed calls loadEverything() at :1699 before reuse.

## L.37 The warp hit test is written twice, and selects the warp through two different doors

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/tools/WarpTool.java:83` - **duplication** - effort: small

**What the code does.** onTileClick (83-97) and onTileMouseDown (104-119) contain the same twelve lines of hit-test arithmetic character for character - the imgstartx/imgstarty centring, the `(warp.x - 9f) * 400d/720d * tilemapScale` mapping, the width/height, the bounds test - differing only in what they do on a hit: `form.showEntry(i)` versus `form.setWarp(i); isDownOnWarp = true;`. TriggerTool, the tool next to it doing the identical job, factored exactly this into one private `hitTestList(...)` (TriggerTool.java:69-88) and calls it from both handlers. showEntry (WarpEditForm.java:112-130) writes warpIndex/warp and the fields directly; setWarp (WarpEditForm.java:223-225) goes through `entryBox.setSelectedIndex(index)`.

**What the user gets.** A fix to the mapping arithmetic - the very arithmetic that has to track any change to tilemapScale or the 400/720 transform - lands on one copy, and warps become unclickable on press or on click but not both, which reads as an intermittent bug. Meanwhile one plain click runs the hit test twice and selects the same warp through two different entry points, one of which bypasses the dropdown the other drives.

**Fix.** Lift WarpTool's hit test into one private method the way TriggerTool already does, and give it one selection call. The capability is "which warp is under this point" and it should exist once; the two forms' `showEntry` vs `setWarp` should likewise collapse to one, since a selection that does not move the dropdown is a form showing one warp and naming another.

**Verified.** Confirmed. The two blocks are byte-identical (see evidence above). Not refused by any suite: DuplicateWorkTest's OWNED table (DuplicateWorkTest.java:60-84) carries no marker for this 400d/720d hit-test mapping - its nearest entry, `720f \+ 360f`, is a different constant owned by TileMapPanel.java - and its UNREACHED_CEILING concerns unreached classes, not duplicated blocks; OUTSTANDING.md has no warp hit-test entry. What does exist is behavioural coverage, not a refusal: EditToolGuardsTest.warpToolPicksTheWarpUnderTheCursor (EditToolGuardsTest.java:947-963) drives onTileClick(click(25,35)) and onTileMouseDown(press(25,35)) at the same pixel, so arithmetic landing on only one copy would fail the battery after the fact. That existing detector, plus the absence of any ROM/workspace write on this path, is why I rate it low rather than medium.

## L.38 Opening any GARC reads every entry in full to sniff 64 bytes of each

`src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:113` - **efficiency** - effort: medium

**What the code does.** parse(): `byte[] buffer = new byte[length]; in.read(buffer); boolean isCompressed = allowCompression && sniffLZ11(buffer);` - buffer is the entry's WHOLE stored length, and sniffLZ11 looks at buffer[0..3] plus LZ11.prefixDecodes(buffer, 64). Nothing else uses buffer; the entry object keeps only offset, length and the flag. The archive holds entries of 4.7 MB and 5.5 MB by this file's own measurement (line 171).

**What the user gets.** Every workspace open, and every re-parse after every pack (parse is called again at the end of replaceWith), reads the entire archive off disk and allocates it entry by entry to look at 68 bytes per entry. The user waits for it seventeen times over, once per opened archive.

**Fix.** Read min(length, SNIFF_PREFIX_BYTES) and pass the real stored length to sniffLZ11 separately - the arithmetic check `buffer.length >= minimumStoredLength(declared)` needs the true length, so it must become a parameter rather than buffer.length. Same pass should stop writeRepacked reopening the archive once per unchanged entry (line 560, inside the loop).

**Verified.** Mechanism confirmed at src/ctrmap/formats/garc/GARC.java:113-116 - `byte[] buffer = new byte[length]; in.read(buffer); boolean isCompressed = allowCompression && sniffLZ11(buffer);` - buffer is unused after line 116 (the GARCEntry built at 118-122 stores only offset/length/compressed), and sniffLZ11 (197-205) examines only buffer[0..3], buffer.length and LZ11.prefixDecodes(buffer, 64). Reached on every workspace open: WorkspaceSession.openArchives() (src/ctrmap/WorkspaceSession.java:295-307) constructs a GARC for the 8 REQUIRED types (89-98) and the 8 SNIFF_OFF types (105-109), and GARC.replaceWith (GARC.java:600) re-parses after every pack. HOWEVER the claimed harm is wrong in scale: the 4.7 MB and 5.5 MB entries quoted from line 171 are in a/0/0/8 and a/1/5/2, archives CTRMap never opens. Measured on the dump at <session folder>/RomFS/000400000011C400, the 16 archives actually opened total 173.7 MB (111.6 MB of it FieldData a/0/3/9; MapMatrix is 227 KB, NPCRegistries 42 KB, every trainer/maison archive under 40 KB), and this same file records the owner's measurement of this exact code at GARC.java:191-193: parsing a/0/0/8 - 1.07 GB - "costs about 0.7 s today". So the open reads ~16% of a measurement already judged acceptable, not seventeen multi-second waits. Also note the naive fix is a corruption hazard: LZ11.prefixDecodes uses the array's o

## L.39 Painting one tile repaints all 1,600 tiles of the region, through a linear colour lookup each

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/Tilemap.java:136` - **efficiency** - effort: small

**What the code does.** updateImage(): `for (int x = 0; x < 40; x++) for (int y = 0; y < 40; y++) { g.setColor(colors.colorOf(Bytes.ba2int(rawTileData[x][y]))); g.fillRect(x*10, y*10, 10, 10); }`. SetTool.updateTile (SetTool.java:70-71) calls `tm.updateImage()` and then perfScale for a SINGLE changed tile, and updateTile is driven from onTileMouseDragged (line 51) — i.e. on every mouse-drag event while painting.

**What the user gets.** One tile changes; 1,600 fillRects run on the EDT. colorOf resolves through EditorTileset.getSimpleColor (EditorTileset.java:80), a linear scan over the tileset — the shipped DefaultTileset.mets holds 77 entries (measured from the file header), and the scan does not stop early on an imperfect match — so that is ~123,000 comparisons plus 1,600 Java2D fills per drag event, followed by perfScale re-blitting the whole 400x400 region image. This is the lag users feel dragging the Set tool across a map. TileUndo.java:127 and GeoEditForm.java:595 repay the same cost per step.

**Fix.** Add `updateTile(int x, int y)` that paints the one 10x10 rect and make updateImage() private (or callers-of-record only), so a single-tile edit cannot ask for a full repaint — the refusal is that the cheap call is the only one reachable from the tools. Separately, index EditorTileset by tile binary in a Map instead of scanning the array.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed, at the cited lines. Tilemap.java:140-145 is unconditionally whole-region: `for (int x = 0; x < 40; x++) for (int y = 0; y < 40; y++) { g.setColor(colors.colorOf(Bytes.ba2int(rawTileData[x][y]))); g.fillRect(x*10, y*10, 10, 10); }` — 1,600 colour lookups + 1,600 fillRects, and Tilemap exposes no per-tile repaint method (its only public painters are updateImage() at :136 and getImage() at :148). SetTool.updateTile calls exactly that for ONE tile: SetTool.java:69-72 `tm.setTileData(lx, ly, actTileData); TileUndo.record(...); tm.updateImage(); host.map().perfScale(..., hilightTileX/40, hilightTileY/40);`, and it is driven from SetTool.java:52-57 onTileMouseDragged, which is reached from TilemapPanelInputManager.java:65-67 `public void mouseDragged(MouseEvent e) { moveSelector(e); tools.current().onTileMouseDragged(e); }` — a Swing MouseMotionListener, i.e. the EDT. The lookup is a linear scan: EditorTileset.java:82 `for (int i = 0; i < tiles.length; i++) if ((tiles[i].binary & 0xFF) == (binary & 0xFF))`, and DefaultTileset.mets really holds 77 entries (header bytes 4-7 = 0,0,0,77). No caller mitigates it: the only guard is the no-op check at SetTool.java:67 `if (!Arrays.equals(tm.getTileData(lx, ly), actTileData))`, which merely skips repainting a tile the stroke did not change — every drag event that lands on a NEW tile pays the full rebuild. No suit

## L.42 Selecting a GARC row decodes the whole container twice

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/builder/Builder.java:61` - **efficiency** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `garcFileList.addListSelectionListener((ListSelectionEvent e) -> { contModel.removeAllElements(); ... loadContainer(...); })` (61-79) never checks `e.getValueIsAdjusting()`, so it runs on both the adjusting and the settled event of every click. loadContainer (93-147) walks every subfile, and for each one with BCH magic does `BCHFile bch = new BCHFile(file)` (104) - a full model/texture parse - purely to build the display string, keeping both `bf.raw` and `bf.bchData` for all of them (98-144).

**What the user gets.** Every click on a row in the GARC list parses every model in that region container twice and holds two copies' worth of parsed BCH plus raw bytes. On a region container this is a visible freeze of the panel on a single-click, and the memory is never released until the next selection.

**Fix.** `if (e.getValueIsAdjusting()) return;` at the top of the listener, and defer `new BCHFile(...)` to the point where the type string is actually needed (the type can be told from the header counts without building the model graph).

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed and nothing upstream stops it. src/ctrmap/humaninterface/builder/Builder.java:61-82 registers `garcFileList.addListSelectionListener((ListSelectionEvent e) -> { contModel.removeAllElements(); ... loadContainer(ContainerIdentifier.makeAGFC(decFile, magicarr, Workspace.session())); ... })` with no `e.getValueIsAdjusting()` test anywhere in the lambda (lines 61-82 contain no reference to `e` at all beyond the parameter). ## L.43 TextFile drops what skip() actually skipped, next door to the skipFully() that exists for exactly this

`src/ctrmap/formats/text/TextFile.java:44` - **guard-debt** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `dis.skip(2)` (l.44) and `dis.skip(offsets[i + 1] - (offsets[i] + lengths[i] * 2))` (l.112) ignore the returned count; InputStream.skip may skip fewer bytes and report it. LittleEndianDataInputStream.skipFully(long) exists at line 64 of ctrmap/LittleEndianDataInputStream.java, added (per OUTSTANDING item A) as "the primitive whose absence caused every hand-rolled short read in the tree", and the container and both GARC entry readers were converted to it. This reader was not.

**What the user gets.** A short skip leaves the stream misaligned by a byte or two, and the very next thing that happens is decryption (getNextValue, l.133-138): every remaining line comes out as plausible-looking garbage rather than as an error. Those lines are location names shown in the zone list and warp dropdowns, so the user reads nonsense and has nothing that says why.

**Fix.** Use skipFully at both sites — a fix that landed on the container and the GARC readers and stopped one file short.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed and the path is live, but the claimed harm is overstated. CONFIRMED: TextFile.java:44 is `dis.skip(2);` and TextFile.java:112 is `dis.skip(offsets[i + 1] - (offsets[i] + lengths[i] * 2));` - both drop the returned count; LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:64 does carry `skipFully(long)` documented "Skips exactly n bytes, or throws: skip() may do less and says so"; the reader is reached in the running app (LocationNames.java:79 `textfile = new TextFile(extracted);` <- CtrmapMainframe.java:2329 loadFromGarc on workspace open, names rendered at WarpEditForm.java:36 and ZoneLoadingPanel.java:249); and no suite under src/ctrmap/tests refuses it (DataSafetyGuardsTest.java:955-965 only checks that one of the first 400 lines matches a plain-name regex, which a misaligned table could still satisfy). THREE CORRECTIONS to the census claim. (1) The harm mechanism cannot fire on this stream: TextFile.java:28 is `new LittleEndianDataInputStream(new FileInputStream(f))` - an unbuffered FileInputStream over a regular file, whose skip() is an lseek that advances exactly n for a forward skip (it will even seek past EOF and report n), so no short skip, no misaligned decryption, no "plausible-looking garbage" names. This is a latent contract violation, not a present silent failure. (2) "The container and both GARC entry readers were converted, this reader was not" impli

Slot 2 is the apron count at :433-435. The one place it is read, :515-517 inside the static `buildModel(...)`, prints `" backing walls: " + SKIRTS[0] + " emitted, " + SKIRTS[1] + " skipped as donor-side; aprons " + SKIRTS[2]` — so both printed backing-wall figures are inflated by the ramp-skirt emitted/rejected counts under labels that name backing walls only. ## L.48 TextFile leaks its FileInputStream on every path that is not a clean read

`src/ctrmap/formats/text/TextFile.java:28` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `LittleEndianDataInputStream dis = new LittleEndianDataInputStream(new FileInputStream(f));` (l.28) is closed only at l.115, the last statement of the try. `if (lineCount == 0) { return; }` at l.31-33 returns with the stream open, and the `catch (IOException ex)` at l.116 logs and falls out with the stream open. There is no finally and no try-with-resources. LocationNames.load constructs one of these per call (LocationNames.java:373) and the class comment says the table is reloaded "after anything rewrites the entry (a zone rename, a text save)".

**What the user gets.** On Windows an open handle blocks rewriting or deleting the file. Every zone rename and text save that hits a zero-line or unreadable GAMETEXT entry strands a handle on the staged text file, and a later pack or workspace clean fails on a file the editor itself is holding — with no hint that the location-name loader is the one holding it.

**Fix.** try-with-resources around the stream so no exit path can leave it open. The same shape appears in NPCRegistry; both should close in a finally the compiler enforces.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed, and I could not refute it. `src/ctrmap/formats/text/TextFile.java:28` opens `new LittleEndianDataInputStream(new FileInputStream(f))`; the only close is `dis.close();` at line 115, the last statement of the try that spans the whole constructor (lines 27-118). Two paths skip it: `if (lineCount == 0) { return; }` at lines 31-33, and `catch (IOException ex)` at 116 which only does `Logger.getLogger(TextFile.class.getName()).log(Level.SEVERE, null, ex);` at 117. There is no finally and no try-with-resources anywhere in the constructor. `LittleEndianDataInputStream.close()` (src/ctrmap/LittleEndianDataInputStream.java:92-93) does delegate to `dis.close()`, so 115 is the real and only release. Reachability in live code is confirmed: `LocationNames.load` does `textfile = new TextFile(extracted);` at src/ctrmap/formats/text/LocationNames.java:79 (NOT :373 as the census claimed - that file is only 113 lines, so that citation is wrong), and three non-test callers reach it - Workspace.java:214 on workspace open, CtrmapMainframe.java:2329 "refresh the dropdown name cache" after a rename, TextEditor.java:206 after a text save - matching the class comment at LocationNames.java:21-23. Nothing in the tree guards it. THAT SAID, the claimed harm is overstated and I am downgrading it: `dis` is a local, so the stream is unreachable the moment the constructor returns 

## L.49 TextFile leaks its stream on the empty-file path, which is a success, not a failure

`src/ctrmap/formats/text/TextFile.java:31` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `LittleEndianDataInputStream dis = new LittleEndianDataInputStream(new FileInputStream(f));` at line 28, then at 31-33 `if (lineCount == 0) { return; }` - returning with the stream open. The only `dis.close()` is at line 115, the last statement of the try, so the IOException catch at 116 leaks as well. Reached from LocationNames.java:79, `textfile = new TextFile(extracted);`.

**What the user gets.** A text entry that legitimately holds no lines leaks a handle on the extracted workspace file every time it is read - the ordinary case, not an error - and the leaked file is one Workspace.cleanUnchanged later tries to delete.

**Fix.** try-with-resources, which makes the early return safe by construction. Same guard as the rest of the class.

**Verified.** The code says exactly what is claimed. TextFile.java:28 opens `new LittleEndianDataInputStream(new FileInputStream(f))` inside a bare try with no finally and no try-with-resources; TextFile.java:31-33 `if (lineCount == 0) { return; }` returns past the only close, which is `dis.close();` at TextFile.java:115, the last statement of the try; the `catch (IOException ex)` at TextFile.java:116 logs and returns with the handle open too. The class is reached: LocationNames.java:79 `textfile = new TextFile(extracted);`, called from Workspace.java:214, CtrmapMainframe.java:2329 and TextEditor.java:206. No caller closes anything (LocationNames.load is a one-liner), nothing in OUTSTANDING.md mentions TextFile, no suite under src/ctrmap/tests scans for unclosed streams, and TextFile is not in DuplicateWorkTest's UNREACHED list (lines 87-113). BUT the claimed harm is refuted: this is not "the ordinary case". The only shipped construction site is the location-names GAMETEXT entry, which is never empty in a working game, and if it were, the early return leaves `lines` null so the next LocationNames.getLocName -> TextFile.getLine hits `if (num >= lines.length)` at TextFile.java:122 and throws NPE before any delete matters. The leak is real but sits on error paths (empty header, or the IOException catch at 116 on a truncated entry), not on a success path.

## L.50 EditorTileset never closes the tileset file - on any path, not just a failure one

`src/ctrmap/formats/tilemap/EditorTileset.java:21` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** The File constructor is `load(new FileInputStream(f));` (line 21) and load(InputStream in) (line 31) wraps it in a DataInputStream, returns early on a magic mismatch (line 36 `return;`), falls off the end on success, and catches IOException - and the word "close" does not appear anywhere in the file. Workspace.getTileset() (Workspace.java:80) constructs a new one on every call, which TileMapPanel.java:764 records in its own comment: "Workspace.getTileset() returns a NEW EditorTileset on every call".

**What the user gets.** A user with a custom tileset (`TILESET_DEFAULT == false`) leaks a handle on their .mets file every time the tileset is re-read - including the re-read wired to Workspace Settings at CtrmapMainframe.java:1512. They cannot then move, edit or replace that file from another program while CTRMap is open, and nothing says why.

**Fix.** try-with-resources in load(), which also fixes the InputStream overload used for the bundled default. The battery guard proposed above catches this one too.

**Verified.** EditorTileset.java:21 is `load(new FileInputStream(f));` — the stream is constructed inline, never stored, never returned; load(InputStream) wraps it in a local DataInputStream (line 33) and exits three ways (early `return;` at line 36 on magic mismatch, fall-off-end on success, `catch (IOException ex)` at line 51) with no finally. `grep -c close` on the file returns 0 — the class has no close(), does not implement Closeable, and holds no reference to the stream, so the caller at Workspace.java:85 (`EditorTileset ts = new EditorTileset(f);`) cannot close it either. Reachable: WorkspaceSettings.java:82-83 sets TILESET_DEFAULT=false and TILESET_PATH from a real radio+path field, and CtrmapMainframe.java:1512 re-runs `mTileEditForm.tileset = Workspace.getTileset()` on every apply; TileEditForm.java:77 calls it at construction. Two corrections to the reporter's harm: Java 8's FileInputStream has a finalizer that closes on GC (release is nondeterministic, not never), and the JDK opens with FILE_SHARE_READ|FILE_SHARE_WRITE but not FILE_SHARE_DELETE, so the user can still write/edit the .mets (including saving over it from TileDBWriter.java:549) — only move/rename/delete is blocked. Wider than claimed in one way: the default branch (Workspace.java:92 and :96) leaks the ResourceAccess.getStream classpath stream identically, and TILESET_DEFAULT defaults to true (Workspace.java:67).

## L.51 One non-daemon thread per list selection, none of them cancellable

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/BuildingPaletteDialog.java:277` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** The selection listener does `new Thread(() -> { ... cachedPrefab(e) ... PaintedRegionBuilder.build(...) ... }, "building-preview").start();` on every selection change. Started from the EDT, these inherit non-daemon. The only cancellation is cosmetic - `if (seq != previewSeq[0]) { return; }` inside the invokeLater, after all the work is done - and cachedPrefab is `synchronized`, so they queue.

**What the user gets.** Arrow-keying down a catalog of thousands queues a full dump-cut plus a 40x40 region build per entry, each running to completion behind the class lock long after its result has been thrown away; the palette stops responding to selection for as long as the backlog takes. Because the threads are not daemons, closing the application while one is cutting keeps the JVM alive - the same defect the camera walker was fixed for.

**Fix.** One worker (a single-thread executor or a SwingWorker that is cancelled when a newer selection arrives), marked daemon, and check the sequence number before starting the work rather than after finishing it.

**Verified.** The code says what is claimed, but the headline harm does not hold. VERIFIED: src/ctrmap/humaninterface/BuildingPaletteDialog.java:277 `new Thread(() -> {` inside `list.addListSelectionListener` (line 265, only guard is `ev.getValueIsAdjusting()`), started with `}, "building-preview").start();` at line 316 - no `setDaemon`, no handle kept, no interrupt. The thread body does `cachedPrefab(e)` (line 281) plus a 40x40 `PaintedRegionBuilder.build(...)` (line 288) plus `p.stampGeometry(base, ...)` (line 291), and the ONLY cancellation is at line 306-308, inside `SwingUtilities.invokeLater`, after every one of those: `if (seq != previewSeq[0]) { return; // a newer selection superseded this preview }`. `cachedPrefab` is line 47 `public static synchronized MapPrefab cachedPrefab(BuildingCatalog.Entry e)` and `donorTextures` line 117 is also `static synchronized`, so the backlog serialises on one class monitor - and its own header comment (lines 41-44) says the Map Builder's live-regen worker contends for the same lock. The contention is reachable from the EDT: PaintForm.java:1025 `TilePainterForm.placedSummary(placed)` runs inside `applyAction()` (an EDT handler, it calls `Ui.confirm` two lines later) and TilePainterForm.java:277 calls `BuildingPaletteDialog.cachedPrefab(pl.e)`, so an Apply can block the EDT behind a drained-out preview queue. The project has already fixed this exact p

## L.52 The collision view runs a 300 fps animator that nothing can ever stop, for the whole session

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/GLPanel.java:35` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** In the constructor: `FPSAnimator animator = new FPSAnimator(this, 300, true); animator.start();`. The animator is a LOCAL variable - no field, so no stop() can ever be written for it. Every sibling keeps it and can stop: CustomH3DPreview.java:21/26 (`private final FPSAnimator animator`, 60 fps, `public void stop()` at 35), H3DRenderingPanel.java:96/103 (60 fps, stop() at 272), MapPreview3D.java:31/53 (40 fps, stop() at 177). The panel is built once at startup - CtrmapMainframe.java:574 `GLPanel glPanel = new GLPanel(mCollEditPanel);` - and put in a split pane at line 704.

**What the user gets.** From the moment the window opens until the process exits, a thread asks for a redraw 300 times a second (five to seven times the rate of every other view in the program) whether or not the collision tab is visible and whether or not a map is loaded. That is a continuously busy core: fan noise, and a laptop battery emptied by an editor sitting idle. NPCEditForm.java:1503 records that these animator threads are not daemons, so nothing but the explicit System.exit(0) in the window handler ends this one.

**Fix.** Keep the animator in a final field and give the panel the `stop()` its three siblings have, at the same frame rate as them; stop it when the collision view is not showing. The refusal: since four classes now hold the identical animator+stop pattern, put it in one base or helper that owns the field and exposes start/stop, so a new GL view cannot be written without a handle on its own clock.

**Verified.** The LINE is real, but the reported harm is mostly refuted; only a narrow defect survives. CONFIRMED as written: src/ctrmap/humaninterface/GLPanel.java:35-36 is `FPSAnimator animator = new FPSAnimator(this, 300, true); animator.start();` as a local; the class has no animator field and no stop() (its only dispose is the empty GLEventListener callback at GLPanel.java:51-52). The three siblings do keep a field and a stop(): CustomH3DPreview.java:21/26 with stop() at :34, H3DRenderingPanel.java:96/103 with stop() at :271, MapPreview3D.java:31/53 with stop() at :177. It is built once at CtrmapMainframe.java:574, put in jsp2 at :704, which becomes the "Collision Editor" tab at CtrmapMainframe.java:728. `getAnimator` appears nowhere in src/, so nothing stops it today, and no suite has a rule about it (every "animator" hit under src/ctrmap/tests is scaffolding calling a preview's stop() so the JVM can exit, e.g. NpcEditFormGuardsTest.java:805). REFUTED - "no stop() can ever be written for it": false. javap on the shipped lib/jogl-all.jar shows `public abstract GLAnimatorControl getAnimator()` on com.jogamp.opengl.GLAutoDrawable, and AnimatorBase registers itself on the drawable it is constructed with. `glPanel.getAnimator().stop()` compiles and works today. The animator is unreached, not unreachable. REFUTED - "asks for a redraw 300 times a second whether or not the collision tab is vis

## L.53 The picker dialog is never disposed when closed with the window button

`src/ctrmap/humaninterface/pokepick/PokePickers.java:118` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** `final JDialog dlg = new JDialog(owner, title, JDialog.ModalityType.APPLICATION_MODAL);` (118) with no `setDefaultCloseOperation`. The default is HIDE_ON_CLOSE, so only the Choose (185) and Cancel (188) paths dispose; the title-bar X hides the dialog and leaves it undisposed. Every other dialog in the tree sets it explicitly: AboutDialog.java:31, WorkspaceSettings.java:220, ESPICAControl.java:51, LoadingDialog.java:66.

**What the user gets.** The picker is opened once per cell edit in the trainer, Maison, shop, item and encounter editors - dozens of times in a session. Each close-by-X leaves a hidden window and its native peer alive until the garbage collector gets to it, which on a long editing session is a slow creep the user feels as the editor getting heavier.

**Fix.** `dlg.setDefaultCloseOperation(JDialog.DISPOSE_ON_CLOSE);` at construction. The rule is worth holding rather than remembering: a check that every `new JDialog` in production code sets a close operation is the same shape as the window ceiling DialogSeamTest already enforces.

**Verified.** Confirmed at the line, with one part of the claimed evidence corrected. src/ctrmap/humaninterface/pokepick/PokePickers.java:118 builds `final JDialog dlg = new JDialog(owner, title, JDialog.ModalityType.APPLICATION_MODAL);` and `grep -n "dispose|CloseOperation|WindowListener|WindowAdapter" PokePickers.java` returns exactly two hits in the whole file - line 185 (`dlg.dispose();` inside the Choose runnable) and line 188 (`cancel.addActionListener(e -> dlg.dispose());`). There is no setDefaultCloseOperation and no WindowListener anywhere in the 397-line file, so the JDK default HIDE_ON_CLOSE stands: the title-bar X hides the dialog, `dlg.setVisible(true)` (214) returns, `pick` returns result[0] == -1 (215), and the dialog and its native peer are never disposed. The path is reachable and hot - five production call sites: TrainerEditDialog.java:138, MaisonEditDialog.java:116, ShopEditDialog.java:113, EncounterEditDialog.java:165 (all via installDoubleClickPickers, one dialog per double-clicked cell) and ItemEditDialog.java:358 (PokePickers.pickItem). No suite refuses it: the only test mention of the class is DialogSeamTest.java:238, and that entry is a member of the WINDOWS ledger that counts how many top-level windows a file constructs ("a modal picker: it returns the chosen species to its caller and closes") - a window-count ceiling, not a disposal check; `grep -rn setDefaultClose

## L.54 Every applied update can leave an orphan copy of the file it replaced, forever

`src/ctrmap/update/Updater.java:583` - **resource-leak** - effort: small

**What the code does.** replace()'s fallback renames the old file aside and never removes it: `File aside = new File(to.getParentFile(), to.getName() + ".old-" + Long.toHexString(System.nanoTime())); Files.move(to.toPath(), aside.toPath()); try { Files.copy(from.toPath(), to.toPath()); return; }` - the success path returns with `aside` still on disk. Grepping the tree, ".old-" appears at this one line and nowhere else; sweep() deletes only the staged tree and download.zip (lines 352-354).

**What the user gets.** This is the path taken for exactly the files the method was written for - jpackage's read-only launcher and a jar still mapped by a process - so a bundle update can strew CTRMap-F5.exe.old-1a2b3c4d and friends through the install folder, one set per update, each a full copy. The class opens by promising the opposite: "nothing here is ever visible next to the app ... there is only ever one CTRMap".

**Fix.** Delete `aside` once the copy has succeeded, and have sweep() remove any leftover *.old-* in the install folder at the next launch (the moment nothing holds them), so a machine that was killed mid-apply tidies itself rather than accumulating.

**Verified.** Confirmed at src/ctrmap/update/Updater.java:582-595. The fallback renames the target aside (line 585 `Files.move(to.toPath(), aside.toPath())` where `aside = new File(to.getParentFile(), to.getName() + ".old-" + Long.toHexString(System.nanoTime()))`, lines 583-584), copies the new bytes in (line 587) and returns at line 588 with `aside` still on disk; the only removal of `aside` is on the failure half (line 590 `Files.move(aside.toPath(), to.toPath()); //put it back`). Nothing cleans it later: sweep() at Updater.java:348-355 deletes only `.ctrmap-update/staged` and `download.zip`, and a tree-wide grep for `.old` outside comments hits line 584 alone (no .bat/.ps1/Java consumer of that suffix). Reachable on both apply paths: replace() is called per-file from copyOver() (Updater.java:559), which runs from applyStaged() (line 318) and the bundle's runApply() (line 453) with dst = installDir, so the orphan lands in the visible install folder, contradicting the class javadoc at lines 22-28 ("nothing here is ever visible next to the app ... there is only ever one CTRMap"). The branch exists precisely for the read-only jpackage launcher and still-mapped jars, per the method javadoc at lines 564-568. Extra evidence the delete is safe: copyOver() already saved the same file to backup/childRel at Updater.java:555 before calling replace(), so the aside copy is redundant.

## L.55 The atmosphere read is swallowed whole, so an unreadable area is shown as an area with no fog

`src/ctrmap/CtrmapMainframe.java:1572` - **silent-failure** - effort: small

**What the code does.** refreshSceneFog ends `} catch (Exception ex) { m3DDebugPanel.clearFog(); }`. The try block covers opening the AreaData container (`new ctrmap.formats.containers.AD(Workspace.getWorkspaceFile(...), game)`), AreaEnv.read(ad.getFile(4)) and env.viewFog(TIME_DAY) - so a container that refuses to open, a short subfile 4 or a malformed AreaEnv all produce exactly the same screen as the legitimate "this area draws no fog" branch eight lines above (`if (fog == null) { m3DDebugPanel.clearFog(); }`). ex is never read.

**What the user gets.** An area whose AreaData cannot be read renders with no fog and says nothing. The user cannot tell that from an area that genuinely has none, so the next step is Map > Edit area fog & lighting on a file that could not be parsed in the first place.

**Fix.** Let the two outcomes differ: keep clearFog for the no-fog answer, and on a caught exception say once (per zone, through Ui) that the area's atmosphere could not be read, with Ui.reason(ex). The fog editor reads the same subfile and already refuses in words when it cannot - this viewer should not be quieter than the editor over the same bytes.

**Verified.** The line is there as quoted. src\ctrmap\CtrmapMainframe.java:1572-1574 is literally `} catch (Exception ex) { m3DDebugPanel.clearFog(); }` — `ex` is never read, nothing is logged, nothing is shown — and line 1568, eight lines above, is the legitimate "this area draws no fog" branch calling the SAME `m3DDebugPanel.clearFog()`, so the two outcomes are byte-identical on screen. The failing path is reachable on real data, not only on corruption: AreaEnv.read (src\ctrmap\formats\area\AreaEnv.java:163-166) throws IllegalArgumentException("area env block must be 2944 bytes") when the block is null or short, and AbstractGamefreakContainer.getFile returns null for a container with too few entries (src\ctrmap\formats\containers\AbstractGamefreakContainer.java:216-221) or for a truncated read (line 239, after a SEVERE log). That an area's subfile 4 can be null-or-not-2944 is conceded by the project's own survey: src\ctrmap\tests\AreaEnvTest.java:69 `if (s4 == null || s4.length != AreaEnv.SUB4_LEN) { continue; }` — it skips such areas rather than asserting they do not exist. The fallback `new AD(Workspace.getWorkspaceFile(...), game)` at CtrmapMainframe.java:1558-1560 can also throw IllegalStateException from AbstractGamefreakContainer.open (line 93-95, "is not a readable AD") into the same swallow.
